+&\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}^{-}\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}y\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{a}v^{(+,0)}\right)\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}T_{g}^{(-)}a^{g+\frac{1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(2.1.19)

We have also used here
v^{(\pm,n)}=v^{(\pm,0)}+2\pi\mathrm{i}ny
and
v^{(+,0)}=-v^{(-,0)}
. This expansion is exactly of the form of the WKB expansion discussed in chapter
1
, as expected from the fact that
I_{\gamma}
describes Bessel functions, that come out of a WKB problem with a linear potential.

We conclude this part by giving some coefficients
T_{k}^{(\pm)}
. Those can be computed algorithmically by residue computation, using the generalized binomial theorem to extract the residue. The first terms of the expansion are, simplified in the region
y>1

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &T_{0}^{(+)}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}=-\mathrm{i}T_{0}^{(-)},\\
&T_{1}^{(+)}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}(2y^{2}+3)}{24\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{7}{4}}}=\mathrm{i}T_{1}^{(-)},\\
&T_{2}^{(+)}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}4\left(y^{2}+75\right)y^{2}+81}{1152\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{13}{4}}}=-\mathrm{i}T_{2}^{(-)},\\
&T_{3}^{(+)}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}(1112y^{6}-117684y^{4}-278478y^{2}-30375)}{414720\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{19}{4}}}=\mathrm{i}T_{3}^{(-)}.\end{aligned}

(2.1.20)

Up to a multiplicative constant, the coefficients are the same of the Debye expansions of the Bessel functions. Those coefficients will allow us to rewrite the Debye expansions of the Bessel functions in terms of the asymptotic expansions of integrals
I_{\gamma}(y,a)
by choosing an appropriate
\gamma
path.

2.1.3
The Stokes phenomenon

The steepest descent contours depend on the phase of
a
,
\theta
. For almost any value of
\theta
, the steepest descent paths are as pictured in figure
2.2
: they never present bifurcations, and only cross one saddle point. There is a discrete set of values of
\theta
for which this does not happen. The description of the same path
\gamma
in terms of steepest descent contours is modified when
\theta
crosses those thresholds: this is an example of the
Stokes phenomenon
in the context of steepest descent integrals. As we will see, this will change the asymptotic behaviour of the function
I_{\gamma}
.

The Stokes phenomenon happens when two saddles are on the same steepest descent contour. This happens whenever

\displaystyle\imaginary\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}(v^{(s_{1},n)}-v^{(s_{2},m)})\right)=0,\quad\real\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}(v^{(s_{1},n)}-v^{(s_{2},m)})\right)>0,

(2.1.21)

where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are signs (variables that can only take value
+
or
-
). In this case, the steepest descent contour from
\tau^{(s_{2},m)}
will flow into
\tau^{(s_{1},n)}
and bifurcate at that point. We picture such an example in figure
2.3
.

Figure 2.3:
Stokes phenomenon. At
\theta=0
, the steepest descent contour
\gamma^{(-,0)}
passes through the saddle
\tau^{(+,0)}
. We have chosen
y=5
.

There are different values of
\theta
at which we have a Stokes phenomenon. In general, a Stokes phenomenon for the path
\gamma^{(s_{2},m)}
will happen whenever
\theta=\arg(v^{(s_{1},n)}-v^{(s_{2},m)})
. If we take
y>1
, when
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are different, the situation is similar to figure
2.3
. When
s_{1}=s_{2}
, however, we have a different situation: in that case we can either have
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
or
\theta=-\frac{\pi}{2}
, depending on the sign of
y
. In this case we have a Stokes phenomenon in which many steepest descent paths are conjoined, as pictured in figure
2.4
. Lastly, when
0<y<1
all the saddles are on the same vertical line, so the Stokes phenomenon only happens for
\theta=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
. In this case, a saddle point is conjoined to two different saddles. We picture this situation in figure
2.5
.

Figure 2.4:
Another example of Stokes phenomenon. At
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, the steepest descent contour
\gamma^{(+,0)}
passes through all the saddles saddle
\tau^{(+,n)}
with
n
strictly positive. We have chosen
y=5
.

Figure 2.5:
Another example of Stokes phenomenon. At
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, the steepest descent contour
\gamma^{(+,0)}
passes through the saddles
\tau^{(-,0)}
and
\tau^{(-,1)}
. We have chosen
y=\frac{1}{2}
.

We now compute the effect of the Stokes phenomenon, by observing how the steepest descent paths get deformed when
\theta
crosses a value at which the Stokes phenomenon appears. There are three possible cases, that we will examine separately.

Case 1:
y>1
,
\theta\neq\pm\frac{\pi}{2}

This case has been pictured in figure
2.5
, but we will choose a situation in which the saddles participating in the Stokes phenomenon are not horizontally aligned, to give a more generic picture (even if this does not change the final result). We start by picking

\displaystyle\theta^{*}=\arg(v^{(+,n)}-v^{(-,0)}).

(2.1.22)

We compute the paths of steepest descent after deformation of
\theta^{*}
by a small positive value. We picture the computation in figure
2.6
. We can recover the path before the deformation by combining the two pictured paths. The Stokes phenomenon then amounts to

\displaystyle\gamma^{(-,0)}\to\gamma^{(-,0)}-\gamma^{(+,n)}.

(2.1.23)

This happens for positive and negative values of
n
, and the same thing also happens for the paths
\gamma^{(+,0)}
: after crossing the angle
\arg(v^{(-,n)}-v^{(+,0)})
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{(+,0)}\to\gamma^{(+,0)}-\gamma^{(-,n)}.

(2.1.24)

Figure 2.6:
Computation of the effect of the Stokes phenomenon between
\tau^{(-,0)}
and
\tau^{(+,1)}
. The orange line is
\gamma^{(-,0)}
for
\theta^{*}-\epsilon
(here
\epsilon=0.1
), while the brown path passing through
\tau^{(-,0)}
is the path
\gamma^{(-,0)}
for
\theta^{*}+\epsilon
, and the brown path passing through
\tau^{(+,1)}
is the path
\gamma^{(+,1)}
(that undergoes no Stokes phenomenon, so we do not need to specify the deformation). We have chosen
y=5
.

Case 2:
y>1,\theta=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}

This case is more complicated. Let us start with
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
: we will have to pick a deformation parameter
\epsilon
, and only in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
we will see the full description. We refer to figure
2.7
.

Figure 2.7:
Stokes phenomenon for
y=5
,
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
. On the left, we have the analysis of the positive saddle, while on the right we have the analysis of the negative saddle. We have chosen
\epsilon=0.1
. In orange we have the paths for
\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon
, in brown the paths for
\frac{\pi}{2}+\epsilon
.

Starting for the Stokes phenomenon related to the saddle
\tau_{(+,0)}
, we see that the orange path
\gamma_{(+,0)}
gets deformed into a path that goes below the singularity
\tau_{(-,N)}
, with
N
dependent from
\epsilon
. We don’t need the exact dependency, we only need to know that
N\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Then we can use the path
\gamma_{(-,N)}
to reach the endpoint of
\gamma_{(+,1)}
, that we can use to reconnect the brown path to the endpoint of the orange path. Assigning the correct orientations, the effect of the Stokes phenomenon is then

\displaystyle\gamma_{(+,0)}\to\gamma_{(+,0)}-\gamma_{(-,N)}-\gamma_{(+,1)}.

(2.1.25)

For the saddle
\tau_{(-,0)}
, we see that in order to reproduce the orange path, we first have to go up
M
times, and then use the path from
\tau_{(+,m)}
to cross over the other side. Here
M
and
m
are also functions of
\epsilon
, and also in this case they go to infinity with
\epsilon\to 0
. The Stokes phenomenon is then

\displaystyle\gamma_{(-,0)}\to\sum_{n=0}^{M}\gamma_{(-,n)}-\gamma_{(+,m)}.

(2.1.26)

Even if there is no path describing the limit for
\epsilon\to 0
, we will see that the asymptotic description will be well-defined.

For
\theta=\frac{3\pi}{2}
, we have the same situation with the roles of
\tau_{(+,0)}
and
\tau_{(-,0)}
interchanged.

Case 3:
0<y<1,\theta=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}

When
0<y<1
, all singularities are on the same vertical line. We picture the arrangement in figure
2.8
.

Figure 2.8:
Stokes phenomenon for
y=\frac{1}{2}
,
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
. On the left, we have the analysis of the positive saddle, while on the right we have the analysis of the negative saddle. We have chosen
\epsilon=0.1
. In orange we have the paths for
\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon
, in brown the paths for
\frac{\pi}{2}+\epsilon
.

As can be seen from the picture, the Stokes phenomenon is simpler than in the previous case. For the negative saddle
\tau_{(-,0)}
there is no Stokes phenomenon at all, as the orange and brown paths have the same endpoints at infinity. For the saddle
\tau_{(+,0)}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{(+,0)}\to-\gamma_{(-,1)}+\gamma_{(+,0)}-\gamma_{(-,0)}.

(2.1.27)

Note that in order to keep the continuity of the integration (that requires the same sign for
\gamma_{(+,0)}
on both sides of (
2.1.27
)) we have to flip the overall sign. This is analogous to what happens in
[
1
]
.

At
\theta=-\frac{\pi}{2}
the opposite phenomenon appears. The positive saddle
\tau_{(+,0)}
does not have a Stokes phenomenon, while the contour around the negative saddle has to be modified as

\displaystyle\gamma_{(-,0)}\to-\gamma_{(+,-1)}+\gamma_{(-,0)}-\gamma_{(+,0)}.

(2.1.28)

2.2
Stokes automorphism of the WKB function

Now that we have established a relation between the integral representation (
2.1.1
), steepest descent paths and the Stokes automorphism in that context, we turn to the next step towards our goal. We now link the description of section
2.1
with the concepts of section
1.4
. This will allow us to write Stokes automorphisms for the sectors appearing in our WKB solution.

2.2.1
Integral representation and Borel transform

Our first step is to obtain an asymptotic expansion for the functions
I^{(\pm,n)}
, in order to obtain our link to the WKB solutions.

We rewrite the integrals
I^{(\pm,n)}
as

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,n)}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}v^{(\pm,n)}\right)\int_{\gamma^{(\pm,n)}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}(v(\tau,y)-v^{(\pm,n)})\right)\differential\tau.

(2.2.1)

We focus our attention on the integral, on which we perform the change of variables

\displaystyle v(\tau,y)-v^{(\pm,n)}=s(\tau,y).

(2.2.2)

As a power series,
s(\tau,y)
reads

\displaystyle s(\tau,y)=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}v_{k}(\tau-\tau^{(\pm,n)})^{k},

(2.2.3)

for some coefficients
v_{k}^{(\pm)}
that can be computed by expansion
2
2

2

Explicit computation gives the coefficients as

\displaystyle v_{k}^{(\pm)}=\frac{1}{k!}\begin{cases}\mp\sqrt{y+1}\sqrt{y-1},&k\text{ even}\\
-y,&k\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

(2.2.4)

. Due to the fact that the series starts at
k=2
, we can perform an inversion of the series as

\displaystyle\tau-\tau^{(\pm,n)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}s^{\frac{k}{2}},

(2.2.5)

for a series of coefficients
c_{k}
determined by plugging (
2.2.3
) into (
2.2.5
) and expanding through the generalized binomial theorem to obtain the identity between LHS and RHS. The integral gets transformed into

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,n)}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}v^{(\pm,n)}\right)\int_{\gamma^{*}_{\theta}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s}{a}}\frac{1}{y-\cosh\tau(s,y)}\differential s,

(2.2.6)

where
\tau(s,y)
is either implicitly defined by (
2.2.2
) or explicitly by (
2.2.5
), and we describe the transformed path
\gamma_{\theta}^{*}
in the following way: as on the path of steepest descent
\imaginary s=\imaginary a
and
\real(s/a)>0
, the path
\gamma^{(\pm,n)}
gets transformed in a path going from
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\infty
to
0
and back. The integrand (
2.2.6
) has an expansion in
\sqrt{s}
, so there’s a branch point at
0
and a branch cut to consider, that we orient along the
\theta
direction. We plot this path in figure
2.9
.

Figure 2.9:
Transformed path of steepest descent
\gamma_{\theta}^{*}
after change of variable (
2.2.2
). The path has to be deformed to go around the branch cut that can be put on the direction of integration
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
. The integration goes around the origin (black dot).

As stated, due to (
2.2.5
), the asymptotic expansion of
(y-\cosh\tau(s,y))^{-1}
will contain square roots. We can write the expansion as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{y-\cosh\tau(s,y)}\simeq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{B}_{k}s^{k}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}B_{k}s^{k}.

(2.2.7)

This kind of expansion is present in
[
1
]
. The integration along
\gamma_{\theta}^{*}
gets rid of the coefficients
\hat{B}_{k}
, due to the lack of a square root branch cut and the exponential decay on the path of steepest descent. We are only left with the coefficients
B_{k}
, and the integration can be expanded as

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,n)}\simeq-2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}v^{(\pm,n)}\right)\int_{0}^{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s}{a}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}B_{k}s^{k}\differential s,

(2.2.8)

using the branch cut to transform the integral over the path
\gamma_{\theta}^{*}
into the simple line integral, picking one of the two determinations. The integration of the formal series can now be carried out, obtaining

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,n)}\simeq-2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}v^{(\pm,n)}\right)\sqrt{a}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}B_{k}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+k\right)a^{k}.

(2.2.9)

We give some coefficients of this expansion for
I^{(+,0)}
in the region
y>1
as

\displaystyle-2\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)B_{0}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}},

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle-2\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)B_{1}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}(2y^{2}+3)}{24(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{7}{4}}},

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle-2\Gamma\left(\frac{5}{2}\right)B_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}(4y^{2}(y^{2}+75)+81)}{1152(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{11}{4}}}.

(2.2.12)

As expected, those coefficients coincide with the coefficients of (
2.1.20
), up to normalization. Let us now examine our WKB transseries (
1.4.10
). We will examine the WKB solution to the problem
3
3

3

The additional
g
factor in (
1.4.10
) has been set to
1
for simplicity: it can be added back by a simple coordinate rescaling.

\displaystyle\psi(x+a,a)+\psi(x-a,a)=2(1+x-x_{0})\psi(x,a),

(2.2.13)

so the turning point is located at
x=x_{0}
. In order to bridge between the two problems, we have to set
1+x-x_{0}=y
. The sector
\Phi_{(+,1)}
is given by

\displaystyle\Phi_{(+,1)}\simeq\frac{1}{(1+(x-x_{0}))^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}+\frac{(3+2(1+(x-x_{0}))^{2})}{24((1+(x-x_{0}))^{2}-1)^{\frac{7}{4}}}a+o(a^{2}).

(2.2.14)

Furthermore, the action
A_{1}^{(\pm,n)}
is given by
A_{1}^{(\pm,n)}=v^{(\pm,n)}+2\pi\mathrm{i}n
. As the WKB solution and
I^{(\pm,n)}
solve the same finite difference equation and they are asymptotic to each other, their asymptotic behaviours must be proportional:

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,n)}\simeq C^{(\pm)}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A_{1}^{(\pm,n)}\right)\Phi_{(\pm,1)},

(2.2.15)

where the constant
C
is determined by comparing the first coefficient in each asymptotic expansion, obtaining
C^{(-)}=\sqrt{2\pi\ a}
and
C^{(+)}=-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi}a
.

The asymptotic relation can be upgraded to a function equality, as
I^{(\pm,0)}
is a function that can be computed at each value of
x
. Then, with
\theta=\arg a
, we have

\displaystyle I^{(\pm,0)}=C^{(\pm)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A_{1}^{(\pm,0)}\right)\mathcal{S}_{\theta}\Phi_{(\pm,1)}.

(2.2.16)

2.2.2
Computation of the Stokes automorphism

Using the results of section
2.1
, we are now ready to describe explicit Stokes automorphisms of the WKB sectors. At a Stokes line, there is a difference between the resummation
\mathcal{S}_{\theta^{+}}
and the resummation
\mathcal{S}_{\theta^{-}}
: this difference can be understood by using the discontinuous jumps of the integrals
I^{(\pm,n)}
. As always, we work case by case.

Case 1:
y>1

Let us start with the sector
\Phi_{(-,0)}
, and choose
\theta_{n}=\arg(v_{1}^{(+,n)}-v_{1}^{(-,0)})
. At this value of
\theta
, the integral
I^{(-,0)}
undergoes a transition, from the path deformation (
2.1.23
).
I^{(-,0)}
then changes as

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to I^{(-,0)}-I^{(+,n)}=I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.17)

The Stokes automorphism at
\theta
is defined as
\mathcal{S}_{\theta^{+}}=\mathcal{S}_{\theta^{-}}\circ\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}
. In order to reproduce (
2.2.17
), we must have (using
y-1=x-x_{0}
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}\left(y-1\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}q_{x_{0}}^{n}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,n)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.18)

This gives our first Borel residue for the linear problem:
\mathrm{S}_{(-,0)\to(+,n)}^{(1)}=-\mathrm{i}
. Analogously, we also get
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(+,0)\to(-,n)}=-\mathrm{i}
by reversing the roles of
\Phi_{(-,1)}
and
\Phi_{(+,1)}
.

We now choose
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
. This automorphism has to be done through a limit procedure. We have to select an
\epsilon
to define
\theta^{\pm}=\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\epsilon
and then send
\epsilon\to 0
. We start from the positive sector, where the path deformation is (
2.1.25
). The automorphism in terms of the
I^{(\pm,0)}
is

\displaystyle I^{(+,0)}\to I^{(+,0)}-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}N\right)I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.19)

In the limit
\epsilon\to 0
we have
N\to\infty
. In the exponential the phase of
a
going to
\pi/2
and
y>0
the argument of the exponential is real negative, so
q^{N}\to 0
in the limit. We have

\displaystyle I^{(+,0)}\to I^{(+,0)}-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.20)

The Stokes automorphism for
\Phi_{(+,1)}
is then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}-q_{x_{0}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,1)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.21)

The Borel residues for this transition are then
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(+,0)\to(+,n)}=\delta_{n,1}
for
n>0
. For the other saddle, we have before
\epsilon\to 0
according to (
2.1.26
)

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to\sum_{n=0}^{M}\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}y\right)I^{(-,0)}+\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}m}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.22)

As discussed, in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
both
M
and
m
go to infinity. The positive saddle disappears and the contribution from negative saddles can be summed up to have

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to\frac{1}{1-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)}I^{(-,0)}.

(2.2.23)

For the sector, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}&=\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}=\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,n)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.24)

The Borel residues for this transition are then
\mathrm{S}_{(-,0)\to(-,n)}^{(1)}=-1
for
n>0
. For
\theta=-\frac{\pi}{2}
we have the opposite situation: for
I^{(-,0)}
we have

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{a}y\right)I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.25)

With the phase of
a
being
-\pi/2
, the argument of the exponential is now real and negative, so
\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}N}{a}y\right)
tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
. We are left with

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(-,0)},

(2.2.26)

so the Stokes automorphism for the sector
\Phi_{(-,1)}
is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}-q_{x_{0}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,1)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.27)

We see that
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(-,0)\to(-,n)}=\delta_{n,-1}
for
n<0
. For
I^{(+,0)}
we have

\displaystyle I^{(+,0)}\to\sum_{n=0}^{M}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}+\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}m}{a}y\right)I^{(-,0)}.

(2.2.28)

As before, the limit removes the contribution from the negative saddle, so we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}&=\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,n)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.29)

The Borel residues are then
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(+,0)\to(+,n)}=-1
for
n<0
.

Case 2:
0<y<1

In this case there are only two Stokes automorphisms to examine,
\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}
and
\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{3\pi}{2}}
. For
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, according to
(
2.1.27
)
we have

\displaystyle I^{(+,0)}\to I^{(+,0)}-I^{(-,0)}-\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(-,0)}.

(2.2.30)

I^{(-,0)}
has a trivial automorphism. The Stokes automorphism for the sector is then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}+\mathrm{i}(1+q_{x_{0}})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,1)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.31)

The non zero Borel residues are then
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(+,0)\to(-,0)}=\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(+,0)\to(-,1)}=-\mathrm{i}
. As we can see, the Borel residues jump discontinuously when
y
goes across the turning point
y=1
.

For
\theta=-\frac{\pi}{2}
we have that
I^{(+,0)}
does not change, while

\displaystyle I^{(-,0)}\to I^{(-,0)}-I^{(+,0)}-\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)I^{(+,0)}.

(2.2.32)

The Stokes automorphism for the sector is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}(1+q_{x_{0}}^{-1})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(-,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(-,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}+\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(+,-1)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(+,1)}.\end{aligned}

(2.2.33)

The non zero Borel residues are then
\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(-,0)\to(+,0)}=\mathrm{S}^{(1)}_{(-,0)\to(+,-1)}=-\mathrm{i}
.

2.3
Expansion of the Bessel functions

2.3.1
Debye expansion

In this section, we will use the
y
variable. Furthermore, instead of
q_{x_{0}}
we will use
q=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{a}y\right)
. As we have seen in the previous section, at the end of the computation we can always translate all the
q
into
q_{x_{0}}
for arbitrary turning points.

The Bessel functions
J
and
Y
have an important expansion, called the
Debye expansion
. Those expansions have been discussed very often in literature, and are available in various references (as an example,
[
42
]
). Our exposition will use the nomenclatures established in
[
16
]
. We start by defining the polynomials
U_{k}(p)
, defined recursively as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&U_{0}(p)=1,\\
&U_{k+1}(p)=\frac{1}{2}p^{2}(1-p^{2})U_{k}^{\prime}(p)+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{p}(1-5t^{2})U_{k}(t)\differential t.\end{cases}

(2.3.1)

We give the starting polynomials of this succession:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}U_{0}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}},\\
&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}y^{-1}U_{1}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)=-\frac{2y^{2}+3}{24\sqrt{2\pi}\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{7}{4}}},\\
&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}y^{-2}U_{2}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)=\frac{4\left(y^{2}+75\right)y^{2}+81}{1152\sqrt{2\pi}\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{13}{4}}},\\
&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}y^{-3}U_{3}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)=\frac{1112y^{6}-117684y^{4}-278478y^{2}-30375}{414720\sqrt{2\pi}\left(y^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{19}{4}}}.\end{aligned}

(2.3.2)

We can see that those coefficients coincide with the coefficients
2.1.20
. The recursive definition is very efficient at generating coefficients, so we can use it to test their growth. As expected, the growth is factorial: we plot an example in figure
2.10
.

Figure 2.10:
Growth of the coefficients
y^{-g}U_{g}
. We have chosen
y=10
, so we have to evaluate the coefficients at
\frac{10}{11\sqrt{3}}
. The coefficients arrange on a line asymptotically, meaning that
y^{-g-1}U_{g+1}\simeq gx^{-g}U_{g}
: this is exactly a factorial growth. The Borel-Padé resummation is necessary to extract finite values from the asymptotic expansions in terms of the
y^{-g}U_{g}
coefficients.

Getting the right expansion is now only a matter of normalization, provided by
[
16
]
. The expansions provided in the reference are for the functions
J_{\nu}(\nu z)
and
Y_{\nu}(\nu z)
, with
\nu
going to infinity on the real positive direction. For this expansion, it is more convenient to work with variables
y,a
of (
2.0.10
): we have to identify
y=\frac{1}{z}
and
a=\frac{z}{\nu}
to recover those variables. For
z
and
\nu
real and positive,
a
will be a real variable, so due to the analysis of section
2.1
we will have to take care of the Stokes phenomenon. Furthermore, the expansions depend on the value of
y
, and change between
y>1
and
0<y<1
.

By comparison, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mp\frac{1}{a}(y\operatorname{arccosh}y-\sqrt{y^{2}-1})}\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}(\pm y)^{-g}U_{g}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)a^{g}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(\pm,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(\pm,1)}\end{aligned}

(2.3.3)

in the region
y>1
, and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mp\frac{1}{a}(y\arccos y-\sqrt{1-y^{2}})}\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}(\pm y)^{-g}U_{g}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}y}{\sqrt{y^{2}-1}}\right)a^{g}=\\
&=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A^{(\pm,0)}_{1}\right)\Phi_{(\pm,1)}\end{aligned}

(2.3.4)

in the region
y<1
. As those will be the series entering the Debye expansions, we will be able to write them in terms of our sectors from the WKB algorithm. Following a common convention
[
40
]
, we rename our sectors to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(y,a)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A_{1}^{(\pm,0)}\right)\Phi_{(\pm,1)}

(2.3.5)

for
y>1
, and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(y,a)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{a}A_{1}^{(\pm,0)}\right)\Phi_{(\pm,1)}

(2.3.6)

for
0<y<1
. We will write the Debye expansions in terms of the asymptotic series
\beta_{\pm}
.

Case 1:
y>1

The expansion for
J
is

\displaystyle J_{\frac{y}{a}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\beta_{+}(y,a).

(2.3.7)

The expansion for
Y
as listed on
[
16
]
is

\displaystyle Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\simeq-2\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\beta_{-}(y,a).

(2.3.8)

This naive expansion would ignore subleading contributions. As we have seen,
\beta_{-}(y,a)
cannot be resummed without ambiguity for
\arg a=0
, due to the presence of the Stokes phenomenon. A way to solve this is to deform
a
with a slight imaginary part as
a\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon
, with
\epsilon>0
. Numerical analysis (as detailed in appendix
A
) shows that the correct expansion is
4
4

4

We will still write
a
instead of
a\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon
in the expansion for brevity: it is intended that
a
has to be deformed in expansion (
2.3.9
)
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\simeq&\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(-2\beta_{-}(y,a)\pm\mathrm{i}\beta_{+}(y,a)\right).\end{aligned}

(2.3.9)

In the examined region, the additional term in (
2.3.9
) is a subleading term in the expansion of
y
, due to the fact that the exponential term is suppressed. This subleading term is important in getting all-orders connection formulae. Historically, in ordinary WKB the role of the Bessel functions is played by the Airy functions, and at first the connection formulae were derived neglecting the subleading term in the expansion of the Airy function
\operatorname{Bi}(z)

[
37
]
. It was only in
[
47
]
that the subleading term was considered, and the correct connection formulae were obtained.

In matrix form, those expansions can be summarized as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\pm\mathrm{i}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

Case 2:
0<y<1

This case has a more straightforward analysis, not presenting a Stokes phenomenon on
\arg a=0
. The expansion for
J
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} J_{\frac{x}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\simeq&\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta_{+}(y,a)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta_{-}(y,a)\right).\end{aligned}

(2.3.16)

For
Y
the expansion is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} Y_{\frac{x}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\simeq&\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}(-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta_{+}(y,a)-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta_{-}(y,a)).\end{aligned}

(2.3.17)

In matrix form, those expansions are given by

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

We stress that the asymptotic expansions that we have written are valid in the region
a>0
, up to small deformations to avoid the Stokes phenomenon. As in our WKB problem we have
a=\mathrm{i}g\hbar
(with
g
the linear coupling), we will need to find a way to write an expansion for
a
being purely imaginary, with its phase being
\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
. For this, we will use the concept of Stokes automorphism introduced in subsection
1.4
. In the next subsection, we explain how to do so.

2.3.2
The physical region

We now get to the main result of this chapter, the asymptotic expansion of the Bessel functions in the physical region. As stated, in the WKB problem we will have
a=\mathrm{i}\hbar g
, with
g
the strength of the linear coupling. In physical applications,
\hbar>0
and
g
is a real number that can be positive or negative. We then need an expansion for the Bessel functions for
a
purely imaginary, with arbitrary sign of the imaginary part. We have seen that at
\arg a=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
we have a Stokes phenomenon, so we will need to make small deformations of
\hbar
with an imaginary part, and take the limit for the imaginary part going to
0
.

Non trivial Stokes automorphisms happen at
\theta_{(\pm,n)}=\arg(A_{1}^{(\mp,n)}-A_{1}^{(\pm,0)})
and at
\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
. It is very convenient to write those automorphisms in a matrix form. As we have computed in subsection
2.2.2
, the Stokes automorphisms in the region
y>1
can be written as

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta_{(+,n)}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mathrm{i}q^{n}\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\quad\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta_{(-,n)}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}q^{n}&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1-q&0\\
0&\frac{1}{1-q}\end{array}\right),\quad\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{1-q^{-1}}&0\\
0&1-q^{-1}\end{array}\right),

while in the region
0<y<1
we have the automorphisms

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mathrm{i}(1+q)\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\quad\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}(1+q^{-1})&1\end{array}\right).

Getting the right asymptotic expansions is now only a matter of multiplying the correct matrices. As always, we distinguish between
y>1
and
y<1
.

Case 1:
y>1

We start with
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}
, meaning that
a
is deformed with a small positive real part. We start our Stokes automorphisms from the region in which
\arg a
is a small positive number, where the expansion is given by

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right)

For the argument of
a
going to
\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}
, we encounter singularities at
\theta_{(-,n)}
with
n>0
. We have to multiply the Stokes automorphism matrices to the expansion matrix in the following way:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\to\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta_{(n,-)}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

The product of Stokes automorphisms is given by

\displaystyle\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta_{(n,-)}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{q-1}&1,\end{array}\right)

so the final expansion for
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}
is

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\frac{q+1}{q-1}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

For
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{+}
, we just have to cross the line at
\frac{\pi}{2}
: to do so, it is sufficient to multiply the matrix that we just obtained by
\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}^{-1}
on the right, obtaining for
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{+}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-q+1&0\\
\mathrm{i}(q+1)&\frac{2}{q-1}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

For
\arg a=-\frac{\pi}{2}^{+}
, we start with
\arg a
small and negative and cross the singularities
\theta_{(n,-)}
with
n<0
. As we are crossing them clockwise, we have to introduce an additional
-1
, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\to\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta_{(-n,-)}}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

The expansion for
\arg a=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{+}
is then

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}\frac{q^{-1}+1}{q^{-1}-1}&-2\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

,
For
\arg a=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{-}
, we also cross the Stokes line at
-\frac{\pi}{2}
via multiplication by
\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{-1}
on the right, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(y^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-q^{-1}+1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}(1+q^{-1})&\frac{2}{q^{-1}-1}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

We note that (
2.3.2
) and (
2.3.2
) can be obtained from (
2.3.2
) and (
2.3.2
) respectively by changing
q\to q^{-1}
and inverting the sign of the off diagonal element.

Case 2:
0<y<1

In this case we only have two non trivial Stokes automorphisms. The matrix multiplications work the same way as in the case
y>1
, so we will directly provide results.

For
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}
there is no Stokes automorphism when starting from
\arg a=0
. The expansion is then

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

For
\arg a=\frac{\pi}{2}^{+}
, we multiply by the appropriate
\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}
, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}q\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(2+q)\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

For
\arg a=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{+}
there is no Stokes automorphism starting from
\arg a=0
, so we have again

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

Lastly, for
\arg a=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{-}
we have

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\\
Y_{\frac{y}{a}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)\end{array}\right)\simeq\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-y^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}q^{-1}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(2+q^{-1})&-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(y,a)\\
\beta_{-}(y,a)\end{array}\right).

Chapter 3
The connection problem

We now come to the main result of this thesis, the computation of connection formulae for the deformed Schrödinger equation. In the resummation of the WKB wavefunctions from the asymptotic series generated by the algorithm of chapter
1
, we have to impose continuity when crossing the turning points. The connection formulae are a tool to impose this continuity and obtain a smooth wavefunction on
\mathbb{R}
, and will allow us to obtain the normalizable spectrum for a large class of potentials, exactly or up to exponentially decaying corrections.

In this chapter, we present a review of the connection formulae of quantum mechanics and their derivation, as we will work by making parallels with the derivation in the standard case. We follow this review with a section dedicated to getting those formulae, and then conclude with a comparison between the two sets of formulae, showing that the formulae of standard quantum mechanics can be recovered in the
\Lambda\to\infty
limit of deformed quantum mechanics. In the review we mainly follow
[
47
]
,
[
40
]
and
[
32
]
.

3.1
Review: connection formulae of standard quantum mechanics

3.1.1
The linear problem: Airy functions and asymptotic expansion

The derivation of the connection formulae starts with the analysis of the linear problem. The standard quantum mechanics eigenvalue problem for the linear potential is

\displaystyle-\hbar^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(x,\hbar)=2x\psi(x),

(3.1.1)

and the linear problem is solved by the Airy functions
\operatorname{Ai}
and
\operatorname{Bi}
as

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=A\operatorname{Ai}\left(-\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}2^{\frac{1}{3}}x\right)+B\operatorname{Bi}\left(-\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}2^{\frac{1}{3}}x\right),

(3.1.2)

with
A
and
B
constants. The Airy functions play the same role that the Bessel functions play in the deformed Schrödinger equation. Their asymptotic expansions can be written in terms of the series

\displaystyle\beta(\nu)=\mathrm{e}^{\nu}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}\nu^{k},\quad c_{k}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\Gamma\left(k+\frac{5}{6}\right)\Gamma\left(k+\frac{1}{6}\right)}{2^{k}k!}:

(3.1.3)

by defining the function

\displaystyle\zeta(z)=\frac{2}{3}z^{\frac{3}{2}},

(3.1.4)

the asymptotic expansions of the Airy functions can be written in the following way: in the region
z>0
they have an exponential behaviour, given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ai}(z)\simeq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta(-\zeta(z)),

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bi}(z)\simeq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{\frac{1}{4}}}(2\beta(\zeta(z))\pm\mathrm{i}\beta(-\zeta(z))),

(3.1.6)

while in the region
z<0
they have an oscillatory behaviour, given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ai}(z)\simeq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}(-z)^{\frac{1}{4}}}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta(\mathrm{i}\zeta(-z))+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta(-\mathrm{i}\zeta(-z))),

(3.1.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bi}(z)\simeq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}(-z)^{\frac{1}{4}}}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta(\mathrm{i}\zeta(-z))+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta(-\mathrm{i}\zeta(-z))).

(3.1.8)

As in the Bessel case, there is a formula with a sign ambiguity: as at
\arg z=0
we have a Stokes phenomenon, we have to deform
z
with a small imaginary part to have a well defined expansion. The
+
sign is chosen for a deformation
z\to z-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
, the
-
sign is used for the other deformation.

3.1.2
Uniform WKB

The procedure to obtain connection formulae is based on
uniform WKB
, that makes a variable transformation in the WKB problem, deforming the non linear potential to the linear one.

The standard WKB ansatz is given by

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right).

(3.1.9)

where
S(x,\hbar)
is a series in
\hbar^{2}
that obeys the Riccati equation

\displaystyle(S^{\prime}(x,\hbar))^{2}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\{S(x),x\}=2(E-V(x)),

(3.1.10)

with

\displaystyle\{S(x),x\}=-2\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}\frac{\differential}{\differential x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}

(3.1.11)

the Schwarzian derivative. The choice of the exponential function is a convenient one, but this is not the only possibility. In the uniform WKB method, the exponential is substituted by an arbitrary function
f
, and the WKB ansatz is given by

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}f(\phi(x,\hbar)).

(3.1.12)

The
\phi
function plays the role of the deformed quantum action in this ansatz. We impose that
f
solves the equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\Pi(\phi)^{2}f(\phi)=0,

(3.1.13)

for a function
\Pi(\phi)^{2}
that we can choose arbitrarily. The reason for this is that (
3.1.13
) is a Schrödinger equation with an arbitrary potential, that can be fixed to suit our needs. The cost for this freedom is a rather complicated equation for
\phi(x,\hbar)
, that is also influenced by the choice of
\Pi
. The function
\phi(x,\hbar)
will solve

\displaystyle\Pi(\phi)^{2}(\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar))^{2}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\{\phi(x,\hbar),x\}=2(E-V(x)).

(3.1.14)

We can recover the standard WKB ansatz by choosing
\Pi(\phi)^{2}=1
, but we will make a different choice. We choose
\Pi(\phi)^{2}=\phi
, so
f
is given by

\displaystyle f(\phi)=A\operatorname{Ai}(-2^{\frac{1}{3}}\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}\phi)+B\operatorname{Bi}(-2^{\frac{1}{3}}\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}\phi).

(3.1.15)

By doing so, we have effectively transformed the WKB problem with arbitrary potential in the linear WKB problem, at the cost of obtaining a non trivial differential equation for
\phi
:

\displaystyle\phi(x,\hbar)(\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar))^{2}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\{\phi(x,\hbar),x\}=2(E-V(x)).

(3.1.16)

We now assume that
x_{0}
is a point for which
E=V(x_{0})
and perform a series expansion of
\phi(x,\hbar)
in even powers of
\hbar
as

\displaystyle\phi(x,\hbar)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\phi_{n}(x)\hbar^{2n}.

(3.1.17)

The zero order satisfies

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x)=\left(\frac{3}{2}\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\sqrt{2(E-V(t))}\differential t\right)^{\frac{2}{3}},

(3.1.18)

and the generic equation for
\phi_{n}(x)
will be of the form

\displaystyle(\phi_{0}^{\prime}(x))^{2}\phi_{n}(x)+2\phi_{0}(x)\phi_{0}^{\prime}(x)\phi_{n}^{\prime}(x)+\dots=0,

(3.1.19)

with
\dots
indicating terms involving all the
\phi_{m}(x)
with
m<n
. The equations for the
\phi_{n}
are then linear non homogeneous equations, and we can choose the integration constants in such a way that
\phi_{n}(x_{0})=0
for
n>0
. Furthermore, we will have
\phi(x,\hbar)>0
for
x>x_{0}
and
\phi(x,\hbar)<0
for
x<x_{0}
.
\phi(x,\hbar)
can then be thought as the necessary coordinate modification to express the solution to the standard WKB Schrödinger equation in terms of the Airy functions.

3.1.3
Uniform WKB and standard WKB

The next step is to connect the standard WKB ansatz

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)

(3.1.20)

to the
\beta
functions that appear in the expansion of the Airy functions. To do so, we first define

\displaystyle\beta_{\pm}=\beta\left(\pm\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{3\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)

(3.1.21)

and then take a different approach to the linkage: we
define

\displaystyle S(x,\hbar)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\log\left(\frac{\beta_{+}}{\beta_{-}}\right)

(3.1.22)

and then show that the
S
defined in this way can be used as quantum actions to solve the Schrödinger equation. In order to do so, we first compute

\displaystyle S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)=\frac{\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar)\sqrt{\phi(x,\hbar)}}{2}\frac{\beta_{+}^{\prime}\beta_{-}+\beta_{+}\beta_{-}^{\prime}}{\beta_{+}\beta_{-}}.

(3.1.23)

The numerator can be evaluated using Wronskian identities, giving

\displaystyle\beta_{+}^{\prime}\beta_{-}+\beta_{+}\beta_{-}^{\prime}=2,

(3.1.24)

so we conclude

\displaystyle S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)=\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar)\sqrt{\phi(x,\hbar)}\frac{2}{\beta_{+}\beta_{-}}.

(3.1.25)

We immediately have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}\phi(x,\hbar)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}.

(3.1.26)

As the RHS is a linear combination of
\operatorname{Ai}
and
\operatorname{Bi}
solving the Schrödinger equation, the LHS also does, so
S
is a quantum action. Furthermore, when the expansion is taken around a turning point at
x=x_{0}
,
S
will be normalized at
x_{0}
, fixing the normalization.

3.1.4
Connection formulae

We are now ready to obtain connection formulae for the standard Schrödinger equation. We will assume that
x_{0}
is such as
E=V(x_{0})
, for
x>x_{0}
we have
E>V(x)
and for
x<x_{0}
we have
E<V(x)
: the classically allowed region is on the right of
x_{0}
, while the classically forbidden region is on the left of
x_{0}
. We will call the classically allowed region
II
and the classically forbidden region
I
, as illustrated in
3.1
.

Figure 3.1:
Classically forbidden (region
I
) and allowed (region
II
) zones for the standard WKB connection formulae.

We use the uniform WKB ansatz with
\Pi(\phi)^{2}=\phi
to write the wavefunction as

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi(x,\hbar)}}(A\operatorname{Ai}(-2^{\frac{1}{3}}\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}\phi)+B\operatorname{Bi}(-2^{\frac{1}{3}}\hbar^{-\frac{2}{3}}\phi)).

(3.1.27)

We start from the classically allowed region
II
, where
\phi(x,\hbar)>0
. Using the asymptotic formulae, the expansion is given by

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi^{\prime}}\phi^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left[(B-\mathrm{i}A)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta\left(\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{3\hbar}\phi^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)+(B+\mathrm{i}A)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\beta\left(-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{3\hbar}\phi^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)\right].

(3.1.28)

Using (
3.1.26
), we have

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left[\left(B-\mathrm{i}A\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S+\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\left(B+\mathrm{i}A\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\right].

(3.1.29)

In the classically forbidden region we have

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi^{\prime}}(-\phi)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left[2B\beta\left(\frac{2}{3\hbar}(-\phi)^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)+(A\pm\mathrm{i}B)\beta\left(-\frac{2}{3\hbar}(-\phi)^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)\right],

(3.1.30)

according to the deformation of
\hbar
(
+
sign for
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
,
-
sign otherwise). In order to deal with the signs coming from the roots of negative quantities, we define in the forbidden region

\displaystyle T(x,\hbar)=\mathrm{i}S(x,\hbar),

(3.1.31)

that is a real function, for which we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}\exp\left(\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)=(-\mathrm{i}T^{\prime}(x,\hbar))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)}.

(3.1.32)

In this region, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\phi^{\prime}}(-\phi)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta\left(\pm\frac{2}{3\hbar}(-\phi)^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{(-T^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}T}.

(3.1.33)

Expansion (
3.1.30
) then reads

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\frac{1}{(-T^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left[2B\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)+(A\pm\mathrm{i}B)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)\right].

(3.1.34)

(
3.1.29
) and (
3.1.34
) allow us to link the two regions, dictating the changes in the multiplicative constants that are necessary to get a continuous asymptotic expansion. Getting the transition formulae is now only a matter of choosing appropriate
A
and
B
to isolate the various exponentials: for the
+
deformation, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right),

(3.1.35)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right),

(3.1.36)

while for the
-
deformation we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right),

(3.1.37)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right).

(3.1.38)

This concludes the derivation for the formulae when
x>x_{0}
is a classically allowed region. We can obtain the other case easily by repeating the same procedure, obtaining the other set of formulae: for the
+
determination, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right),

(3.1.39)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right),

(3.1.40)

while for the
-
determination we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right),

(3.1.41)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S\right)\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right).

(3.1.42)

This concludes the derivation of the WKB connection formulae for standard quantum mechanics. We will follow this procedure closely in deriving the formulae for deformed quantum mechanics, exploiting the asymptotic expansion of the Bessel functions that was obtained in chapter
2
.

3.2
Connection formulae of deformed quantum mechanics

3.2.1
Turning points

The first main difference between the standard WKB ansatz and the deformed WKB ansatz is the doubling of turning points. While in standard WKB we have turning points for all
x_{0}
such as
E=V(x_{0})
, the turning points of the deformed WKB ansatz are given by
E=V(x_{0})
and
E=V(x_{0})-2
, as seen in chapter
1
. We call the turning points
x_{0}
such as
E=V(x_{0})

turning points at
1
, while the turning points at
x_{0}
will be called
turning points at
-1
. The reason for this nomenclature comes by examining the finite difference Schrödinger equation

\displaystyle\psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=2(E-V(x)+1)\psi(x,\hbar):

(3.2.1)

at the turning points at
1
, the bracket on the RHS has value
1
, while at the turning points at
-1
it has value
-1
.

In chapter
2
, we have only expanded the Bessel functions around a turning point at
1
. No other expansion is needed, as the expansions for the turning points at
-1
can be obtained from the expansions at the turning points at
-1
. In fact, assume that
x_{0}
is a turning point at
-1
: the function
\varphi(x,\hbar)=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}(x-x_{0}+1)\right)\psi(x,\hbar)
solves

\displaystyle\varphi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\varphi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=-2(E+V(x)-1)\varphi(x,\hbar).

(3.2.2)

For the function
\varphi
,
x_{0}
is now a turning point at
1
. This trick has been vastly used in the works on finite difference WKB (see
[
43
]
and
[
20
]
as examples). We can focus on the turning points at
1
, and then obtain the connection formulae for the turning points at
-1
by using this technique.

From now on, we will assume that
x_{0}
is a turning point at
1
for the WKB ansatz, with the added imposition of the turning point being
simple
, meaning
V^{\prime}(x_{0})\neq 0
at the turning point.

3.2.2
From the linear problem to a uniform WKB ansatz

As we have seen, the most generic linear problem can be written as

\displaystyle\psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=2(E-gx+1)\psi(x,\hbar),

(3.2.3)

where
g
is a coupling constant. It is convenient to rewrite this as

\displaystyle\psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=2(1-g(x-x_{0}))\psi(x,\hbar),

(3.2.4)

with
x_{0}=E/g
. The solution to this difference equation is

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=A(q_{x_{0}})J_{\frac{1-g(x-x_{0})}{\mathrm{i}g\hbar}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}g\hbar}\right)+B(q_{x_{0}})Y_{\frac{1-g(x-x_{0})}{\mathrm{i}g\hbar}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}g\hbar}\right),

(3.2.5)

where we recall that
q_{x_{0}}
is defined as

\displaystyle q_{x_{0}}=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}(x-x_{0})\right),

(3.2.6)

and
A
and
B
are Laurent series in
q_{x_{0}}
. This solution can be used to build an ansatz for the general problem.

For the general problem (
3.2.2
), we would have to follow the procedure of standard WKB, obtaining formulae corresponding to (
3.1.13
) and (
3.1.14
). Unfortunately, getting those equations require usage of the chain rule for ordinary derivatives, and we do not have such a tool at our disposal in the deformed case. We can skip this step, at the cost of not having a well-defined equation for the deformation
\phi(x,\hbar)
. While this is surely problematic from a theoretical point of view (as an equation would help establish existence of
\varphi
), the final result will be independent of the particular deformation. Here lies the principal conjecture of this thesis: we will assume existence of this deformation, and test our results numerically to give a motivation for our conjecture.

We start from the classically allowed region, assuming that
x>x_{0}
is the allowed region. The deformed WKB ansatz with the even-odd relation implemented is

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)\exp\left(\mp\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right).

(3.2.7)

Here we have denoted
1
1

1

The operator
D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}
is known as a
finite difference derivative
. We won’t go into details in finite difference derivatives, but the reader can find details on
[
38
]
and
[
30
]
.

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)=\frac{S\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)-S\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}.

(3.2.8)

In order to reproduce the uniform WKB ansatz in the deformed case, we define

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)=&\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\times\\
&\times\frac{1}{(\phi(x,\hbar)^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(\phi(x,\hbar),-\mathrm{i}\hbar V^{\prime}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3.2.9)

\beta_{\pm}
are the series defined by (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
), used for the asymptotic expansion of the Bessel functions. The factor
V^{\prime}(x)
in the second argument of
\beta
is necessary, as in (
3.2.5
) we see that
\hbar
is always multiplied by minus the slope of the potential at
x=x_{0}
. As in the ordinary WKB ansatz,
\phi(x,\hbar)
is a deformation needed to have
\varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)
as a solution to (
3.2.2
), and
\phi(x,\hbar)
is a series of the form

\displaystyle\phi(x,\hbar)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hbar^{2n}\phi_{n}(x).

(3.2.10)

\phi_{0}(x)
is implicitly defined by

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{V^{\prime}(x)}\int_{1}^{\phi_{0}(x)}\operatorname{arccosh}t\differential t=\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1)\differential t,

(3.2.11)

in order to make the exponential terms match. From this equation, we have that
\phi_{0}(x)>1
in the allowed region,
0<\phi_{0}(x)<1
in the forbidden region. The equation for the higher terms is as before a linear non homogeneous differential equation of first order, so we can always set
\phi_{n}(x_{0})=0
for
n>0
. The difference with standard WKB is that the only way that we have found to compute the
\phi_{n}(x)
for
n>0
is to take a series expansion in
\hbar
of the coefficients of
\beta_{\pm}
and then match coefficients of the expansion. As stated, the existence of this function is the main conjecture of this thesis, but no details of
\phi
will be needed in the practical computations.

As before, the linkage is made by forgetting for a moment the definition of
S(x,\hbar)
in terms of chapter
1
, and redefining

\displaystyle S(x,\hbar)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\log\frac{\varphi_{+}(x,\hbar)}{\varphi_{-}(x,\hbar)}.

(3.2.12)

We have to simplify the even-odd term in (
3.2.7
). We now compute

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\varphi_{+}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}{\varphi_{-}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}\frac{\varphi_{-}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}{\varphi_{+}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}

(3.2.13)

The application of
\sinh
gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)=\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\varphi_{-}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)-\varphi_{-}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}{\sqrt{\varphi_{-}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{-}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)}}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.14)

The term in the numerator is the
Casoratian
of (
3.2.2
), as
\varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)
solve the general problem. The Casoratian is the equivalent of the Wronskian for difference equations
[
54
]
. The Casoratian is zero if and only if
\varphi_{-}
and
\varphi_{+}
can be obtained from one another by multiplication by a periodic function, and is a non zero periodic function otherwise. As
\varphi_{-}
and
\varphi_{+}
are independent solutions (as evident from their asymptotics), we conclude that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{-}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)-&\varphi_{-}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)=\\
&=2P\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)^{-2},\end{aligned}

(3.2.15)

where
P
is a periodic function in
x
of period
\mathrm{i}\hbar
and the additional factors are chosen for convenience. Continuing the computation, we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)=-2\log P\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)-\\
-&\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\varphi_{-}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{-}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\varphi_{+}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.16)

We now have to apply the operator
\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}
. To do so, we check the action of its inverse on test functions
f(x,\hbar)
:

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)f(x,\hbar)=\frac{1}{2}\left(f\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)+f\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2},\hbar\right)\right).

(3.2.17)

If
f
has period
\mathrm{i}\hbar
in
x
, the operator is a simple translation, so the action of its inverse on
P(x,\hbar)
is trivial. Due to the properties of
\log
, the second row of (
3.2.16
) is exactly of the form of the RHS of (
3.2.17
): we can then conclude that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S=\log P\left(x,\hbar\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\varphi_{+}(x,\hbar)\varphi_{-}(x,\hbar).

(3.2.18)

From this, we can conclude

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\exp\left(\mp\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)=\\
=&P(x,\hbar)\varphi_{+}(x,\hbar)\varphi_{-}(x,\hbar)\left(\frac{\varphi_{+}(x,\hbar)}{\varphi_{-}(x,\hbar)}\right)^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}=P(x,\hbar)\varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar).\end{aligned}

(3.2.19)

We can conclude that
\psi_{\pm}
defined in (
3.2.7
) with the redefinition (
3.2.12
) is a solution of (
3.2.2
), as
\varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)
are solutions to (
3.2.2
). We can expand
P
as

\displaystyle P(x,\hbar)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}p_{n}(\hbar)\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}x\right):

(3.2.20)

in order to have (
3.2.11
), due to the fact that the imaginary part of
\operatorname{arccosh}
in the chosen determination can only go from
-\mathrm{i}\pi
to
\mathrm{i}\pi
, we can assume that
p_{n}(x)=0
for all
n\neq 0
, so
P(x,\hbar)
will reduce to a constant that is not important in our work.

We now have to consider other possible solutions, the ones obtained by multiplying the standard solution (
3.2.7
) by
q_{x_{0}}
. This can be done by translating the
\operatorname{arccosh}
functions in (
3.2.11
) by
2\pi\mathrm{i}
, obtaining

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{V^{\prime}(x)}\int_{1}^{\phi_{0}(x)}\operatorname{arccosh}t\differential t-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}n}{V^{\prime}(x)}(\phi_{0}(x)-1)=\\
=&\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1)\differential t+2\pi\mathrm{i}n(x-x_{0}),\end{aligned}

(3.2.21)

with
n
an integer. Expanding
\phi_{0}(x)
to first order in
x-x_{0}
shows that at least at this order
\phi_{0}(x)
satisfies the updated equation. We naturally come to the definition of the equivalents of the
q_{x_{0}}
factors in the uniform deformed WKB ansatz:

\displaystyle q^{(\phi)}=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi n}{\hbar V^{\prime}(x)}(\phi_{0}(x)-1)\right).

(3.2.22)

Our conclusion is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\exp\left(\mp\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)q_{x_{0}}^{\pm n}=\\
=&(q^{(\phi)})^{\pm n}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\times\\
&\times\frac{1}{(\phi(x,\hbar)^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(\phi(x,\hbar),-\mathrm{i}\hbar V^{\prime}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3.2.23)

This is the conclusion in the classically allowed region to bridge between the deformed WKB ansatz and the uniform WKB ansatz adapted to the deformed case.

In the forbidden region (that we assume to be at
x<x_{0}
),
S(x,\hbar)
is imaginary, so it is more convenient to work with the real function

\displaystyle T(x,\hbar)=\mathrm{i}S(x,\hbar).

(3.2.24)

\psi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)
becomes

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log(-\ii\sin D_{\ii\hbar} T(x,\hbar))\right)\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right).

(3.2.25)

\phi_{0}
is still defined by (
3.2.11
), and is smaller than
1
in the forbidden region. We rewrite that equation as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{V^{\prime}(x)}\int_{\phi_{0}(x)}^{1}\arccos t\differential t=\int_{x}^{x_{0}}\arccos(E-V(t)+1)\differential t,

(3.2.26)

As in the forbidden region the argument of the
\arccos
in the RHS is between
-1
and
1
, this is a real valued equality, and
\phi_{0}(x)<1
. We then rewrite
\varphi_{\pm}
as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{\pm}(x,\hbar)=&\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\times\\
&\times\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{(1-\phi(x,\hbar)^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(\phi(x,\hbar),-\mathrm{i}\hbar V^{\prime}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3.2.27)

In (
3.2.25
), we can extract the
-\mathrm{i}
from the
\log
to obtain a phase of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
. Following the same procedure of the allowed zone, we conclude

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sin D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T\right)\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)=\\
=&\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\frac{1}{(1-\phi(x,\hbar)^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(\phi(x,\hbar),-\mathrm{i}\hbar V^{\prime}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3.2.28)

For the other determinations, as we can recover them from
S\to S+2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-x_{0})
, we have to impose
T\to T-2\pi(x-x_{0})
. This we can do by shifting the
\arccos
in (
3.2.26
) by
-2\pi n
. Following the same steps, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sin D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T\right)\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)q_{x_{0}}^{\pm n}=\\
=&(q^{(\phi)})^{\mp n}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\times\\
&\times\frac{1}{(\phi(x,\hbar)^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\beta_{\pm}(\phi(x,\hbar),-\mathrm{i}\hbar V^{\prime}(x)).\end{aligned}

(3.2.29)

There is an important difference between (
3.2.23
) and (
3.2.29
): the exponents of
q^{(\phi)}
have inverted sign. This will be very important in determining the correct transition formulae. Now that we have established the bridges between the two descriptions, we can obtain the formulae.

3.2.3
Computation of deformed WKB transition formulae

We now come to the main result. We will assume that
x_{0}
is a turning point at
1
, with
V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0
so the classically allowed region is for
x>x_{0}
and the classically forbidden region is for
x<x_{0}
. We picture the arrangement in figure
3.2

Figure 3.2:
Turning point, allowed region and forbidden region in deformed WKB. We also picture (without label) the turning point at
-1
, delimited by the line
V(x)=E+2
.

The wavefunction can be written in the uniform deformed WKB ansatz as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \psi(x,\hbar)=&\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi(x,\hbar)\right)\times\\
&\times\left(A(q^{(\phi)})J_{-\frac{\phi(x,\hbar)}{V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar}}\left(-\frac{1}{V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar}\right)+B(q^{(\phi)})Y_{-\frac{\phi(x,\hbar)}{V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar}}\left(-\frac{1}{V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar}\right)\right)\end{aligned}

(3.2.30)

We can use the expansions obtained in subsection
2.3.2
to obtain the asymptotic expansions of
J
and
Y
. For now we will not use explicit matrices, but denote
M_{A}(q^{(\phi)})
as the matrix for the expansion in the allowed region,
M_{F}(q^{(\phi)}
as the matrix in the forbidden region. The expansion reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\frac{1}{(\phi^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{x}}{2}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\phi\right)\times\\
&\times\begin{cases}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A(q_{\phi})&B(q_{\phi})\end{array}\right)M_{A}(q_{\phi})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(\phi,-V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar)\\
\beta_{-}(\phi,-V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar)\end{array}\right),\quad&x>x_{0},\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A(q_{\phi})&B(q_{\phi})\end{array}\right)M_{F}(q_{\phi})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{+}(\phi,-V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar)\\
\beta_{-}(\phi,-V^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{i}\hbar)\end{array}\right),\quad&x<x_{0}.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

(3.2.31)

Using formulae (
3.2.23
) and (
3.2.29
), we can convert this asymptotic expansion in an expansion in terms of the deformed WKB ansatz as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\times\\
\times&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A\left(q_{x_{0}}\right)&B\left(q_{x_{0}}\right)\end{array}\right)M_{A}\left(q_{x_{0}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S(x,\hbar)\right)\end{array}\right)\end{aligned}

(3.2.32)

in the allowed region, and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\psi(x,\hbar)\simeq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sin D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T\right)\times\\
\times&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A\left(q_{x_{0}}^{-1}\right)&B\left(q_{x_{0}}^{-1}\right)\end{array}\right)M_{A}\left(q_{x_{0}}^{-1}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T(x,\hbar)\right)\end{array}\right)\end{aligned}

(3.2.33)

in the forbidden region. Note the presence of the
q_{x_{0}}^{-1}
in the expansion of the forbidden region, consequence of (
3.2.29
). Obviously, if
V^{\prime}(x)>0
(so
x<x_{0}
is the allowed region) the two regions have to be inverted.

We now give the connection formulae case by case. We have two parameters to account for: the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon
and the sign of the potential
V^{\prime}(x_{0})
.

Case 1:
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0

In this case the allowed zone is at
x>x_{0}
, and
\arg(-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\mathrm{i}\hbar)=\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}
, so the expansions to use are (
2.3.2
) in the allowed region, and (
2.3.2
) for the forbidden region. We write them here
2
2

2

The additional phase of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
comes from the
\sqrt{a}
factor in the expansions, that becomes
\sqrt{\mathrm{i}\hbar}
once
-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar
is plugged in. For
V^{\prime}(x_{0})>0
we will have an additional phase of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
.
:

\displaystyle M_{A}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{-\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(\phi^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}&0\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}\frac{q^{(\phi)}+1}{q^{(\phi)}-1}&-2\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle M_{F}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{-\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\phi^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&-1\end{array}\right).

We can now use (
3.2.32
) and (
3.2.33
) to obtain the expansions for
\psi(x,\hbar)
and connect the solutions. We can choose
A
and
B
in such a way to obtain the connection formulae for the different exponentials. We obtain
3
3

3

For brevity, we denote

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}(x,\hbar)=-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sinh D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S,

(3.2.38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}(x,\hbar)=-\frac{1}{2}\cosh\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\partial_{x}\right)^{-1}\log\sin D_{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T.

(3.2.39)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.40)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)-\frac{1}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.41)

Inverse formulae are obtained in the same way:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.42)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(-\frac{1}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.43)

As the transformations are linear, we can arrange them in matrix form:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&-\frac{1}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.44)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\frac{1}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.45)

(
3.2.44
) and (
3.2.45
) are more compact than (
3.2.40
) to (
3.2.43
), so we will always give results in matrix form. Furthermore, one can verify that the matrix in (
3.2.44
) is the inverse of the matrix in (
3.2.45
) evaluated with
q_{x_{0}}\to q_{x_{0}}^{-1}
: the necessity of the inversion comes from the
-1
factors in (
3.2.33
), not present in (
3.2.32
). This will be a general property.

Case 2:
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})>0

In this case the forbidden region is at
x>x_{0}
and the allowed region is at
x<x_{0}
. As
\arg(-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\mathrm{i}\hbar)=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{-}
, the matrices to use are (
2.3.2
) in the allowed zone and (
2.3.2
) in the forbidden zone. The matrices are

\displaystyle M_{A}(q^{(\phi)})=\sqrt{\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(\phi^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(1-(q^{(\phi)})^{-1})&0\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(1+(q^{(\phi)})^{-1})&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{2}{(q^{(\phi)})^{-1}-1)}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle M_{F}(q^{(\phi)})=\sqrt{\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\phi^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}(q^{(\phi)})^{-1}&1\\
-(2+(q^{(\phi)})^{-1})&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right).

The transitions in matrix form are given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}-1}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.50)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}\\
