\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}-1}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.51)

Case 3:
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0

In this case the forbidden region is at
x<x_{0}
and the allowed region at
x>x_{0}
. As
\arg(-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\mathrm{i}\hbar)=\frac{\pi}{2}^{+}
, we use (
2.3.2
) for the allowed region and (
2.3.2
) for the forbidden region. The matrices are

\displaystyle M_{A}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(\phi^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}(1-q^{(\phi)})&0\\
-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}(q^{(\phi)}+1)&-\frac{2}{q^{(\phi)}-1}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle M_{F}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\phi^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-\mathrm{i}q^{(\phi)}\\
-\mathrm{i}&-(2+q^{(\phi)})\end{array}\right).

The transitions are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}&-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.56)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}}{q_{x_{0}}-1}\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.57)

Case 4:
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})>0

In this case the forbidden region is at
x>x_{0}
and the allowed region at
x<x_{0}
. As
\arg(-V^{\prime}(x_{0})\mathrm{i}\hbar)=\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{+}
, we use (
2.3.2
) for the allowed region and (
2.3.2
) for the forbidden region. The matrices are

\displaystyle M_{A}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(\phi^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}&0\\
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}\frac{(q^{(\phi)})^{-1}+1}{q^{(\phi)})^{-1}-1}&-2\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle M_{F}\left(q^{(\phi)}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{V^{\prime}(x_{0})\hbar}{2\pi}}\frac{1}{(1-\phi^{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathrm{i}&1\\
-1&\mathrm{i}\end{array}\right).

The transitions are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q^{-1}_{x_{0}}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}-1}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.2.62)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to\\
\to&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}}{q_{x_{0}}^{-1}-1}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.2.63)

3.2.4
Turning points at
-1

Up until now, we have studied the turning points at
1
. As anticipated, in order to obtain those transition formulae we can use a trick. If
\psi(x,\hbar)
solves

\displaystyle\psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=2Q(x)\psi(x,\hbar),

(3.2.64)

with a turning point
x_{0}
such as
Q(x_{0})=-1
, then
\Psi(x,\hbar)=\sqrt{q_{x_{0}}}\Psi(x,\hbar)
solves

\displaystyle\Psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\Psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=-2Q(x)\Psi(x,\hbar),

(3.2.65)

so
x_{0}
is a turning point at
1
for
\Psi(x,\hbar)
, and we can use the standard connection formulae there.

In order to implement this, we note that we can implement this transformation by changing the
P_{0}
term as

\displaystyle P_{0}(x)=\operatorname{arccosh}Q(x)\to-\operatorname{arccosh}(-Q(x))+\mathrm{i}\pi:

(3.2.66)

the integration from
x_{0}
to
x
will produce the exact
\sqrt{q_{x_{0}}}
term that we are looking for
4
4

4

Our choice is not the only possibility, as there are other determinations of
\operatorname{arccosh}
that we have ignored. We have made this choice a posteriori, by looking at the example of the Toda lattice that we will study in the next chapter and comparing with the known result. A future line of research is to make this work more rigorous, and justifying this choice among all possibilities could be a future result.
. Obtaining the transition matrices is then very simple: defining

\displaystyle I^{(pm)}(q_{x_{0}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&q^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}_{x_{0}}\\
q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}_{x_{0}}&0\end{array}\right),

where the upper sign is picked for the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
and the lower sign for the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon
all transition matrices at
-1
can be obtained by multiplication on the left and the right by this matrix, also considering the fact that as
Q(x)
is inverted we have to invert the sign of
V^{\prime}(x_{0})
to pick the right matrix.

3.3
Limit to standard WKB

The connection formulae that we have written have to be intended as deformations of the standard WKB connection formulae. In particular, in the limit to standard quantum mechanics, they reproduce the standard WKB connection formulae. We will show that in this section, studying how the deformed WKB ansatz reduces to the standard one in the appropriate limit.

At first, we have to clarify how to get the limit to standard quantum mechanics. As we have seen in chapter
1
, this can be done by restoring the
\Lambda,m
dependency through dimensional analysis, and then sending
\Lambda\to\infty
. We start from (
1.2.2
), that we rewrite for convenience:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}(S_{\text{T}}(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)-S_{\text{T}}(x,\hbar))\right)+&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}(S_{\text{T}}(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)-S_{\text{T}}(x,\hbar))\right)=\\
&=2Q(x),\end{aligned}

(3.3.1)

with
Q(x)=E-V(x)+1
. Here we have reintroduced the total action
S_{\text{T}}
, including even and odd parts in
\hbar
. We first have to make
Q(x)
adimensional: as
\Lambda
has the dimensions of an energy, this is trivially done by

\displaystyle Q(x)=\frac{E-V(x)}{\Lambda}+1.

(3.3.2)

Let us now turn to the argument of the exponentials. We cannot have linear combinations of
x
and
\hbar
as they have different dimensions: exploiting the fact that
\sqrt{m\Lambda}
has the dimension of momentum, we see that the appropriate rescaling is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Lambda\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\left(S_{\text{T}}\left(x+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{\sqrt{m\Lambda}},\hbar\right)-S_{\text{T}}(x,\hbar)\right)\right)+\\
+&\Lambda\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\left(S_{\text{T}}\left(x-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{\sqrt{m\Lambda}},\hbar\right)-S_{\text{T}}(x,\hbar)\right)\right)=2(E-V(x)+\Lambda),\end{aligned}

(3.3.3)

We now just have to expand
\Lambda
around
\infty
to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\left(S_{\text{T}}\left(x\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{\sqrt{m\Lambda}},\hbar\right)-S_{\text{T}}(x,\hbar)\right)\right)=\\
=&1\pm\frac{S_{\text{T}}^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}{\sqrt{m\Lambda}}+\frac{S_{\text{T}}^{\prime}(x,\hbar)^{2}+\mathrm{i}\hbar S_{\text{T}}^{\prime\prime}(x,\hbar)}{2m\Lambda}+o\left(\Lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.3.4)

In the sum of (
3.3.3
), the terms proportional to
\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}
cancel out, and the term
2\Lambda
on the LHS is matched by the one on the RHS. We are left with

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{T}(x,\hbar)+\mathrm{i}S^{\prime\prime}_{T}(x,\hbar)=2m(E-V(x)),

(3.3.5)

that is precisely the starting point for the WKB analysis of the Schrödinger equation. From this we obtain that the
S
of deformed WKB goes to the
S
of standard WKB in the
\Lambda\to\infty
limit, and also the even-odd terms become

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}(x,\hbar)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}}

(3.3.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}T_{o}(x,\hbar)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}(x,\hbar)}},

(3.3.7)

simplifying to the standard WKB expressions.

The exponential term
q_{x_{0}}
also has an important limit. Restoring units, we have

\displaystyle q_{x_{0}}=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi\sqrt{m\Lambda}}{\hbar}(x-x_{0})\right).

(3.3.8)

As
\hbar>0
, the limit of
q_{x_{0}}
for
\Lambda\to\infty
entirely depends on the sign of
x-x_{0}
:
q_{x_{0}}\to 0
for
x>x_{0}
, and
q_{x_{0}}\to\infty
for
x<x_{0}
. With this limit, we are ready to take the standard WKB limit of our connection formulae. As always, we work case by case.

We also note that the only relevant connection formulae are the ones at
x_{0}
such as
E=V(x_{0})
, the turning points at
1
. This is because the other turning points are located at
E+2\Lambda=V(x_{0})
: in the
\Lambda\to\infty
limit, those connection formulae are never encountered.

Case 1:
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0

The transition formulae are (
3.2.44
) and (
3.2.45
). We have
x>x_{0}
in the allowed region and
x<x_{0}
in the forbidden region. As (
3.2.44
) mixes terms in the forbidden region, we have to take the limit
q_{x_{0}}\to\infty
there and
q_{x_{0}}\to 0
in the other matrix. The transition formulae become

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.3.10)

The first transition reproduces (
3.1.35
) and (
3.1.36
) and the second is the inverse of the first, so we have correctly recovered the standard limit.

Case 2:
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})>0

The transition formulae are (
3.2.50
) and (
3.2.51
). This time we have to invert the limits, as
x<x_{0}
is the allowed region and
x>x_{0}
is the forbidden region. We have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&0\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.3.12)

The first matrix coincides with (
3.1.39
) and (
3.1.40
), and the second matrix is the inverse of the first, so this transition also correctly reduces to the standard WKB transition.

Case 3:
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0

Here we use the transitions (
3.2.56
) and (
3.2.57
), with
x>x_{0}
the allowed region and
x<x_{0}
the forbidden region. The appropriate limit is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&0\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.3.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&0\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.3.14)

The first matrix gives transformations (
3.1.37
) and (
3.1.38
), and the second matrix is the inverse of the first, so the standard WKB transition is correctly reproduced.

Case 4:
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon,V^{\prime}(x_{0})<0

The last transitions to use are (
3.2.62
) and (
3.2.63
), with
x<x_{0}
the forbidden region and
x>x_{0}
the allowed region. We have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right),\end{aligned}

(3.3.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\sqrt{-T^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}T\right)\end{array}\right)\to&\frac{1}{\sqrt{S^{\prime}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

(3.3.16)

As always, the first matrix coincides with (
3.1.41
) and (
3.1.42
) and the second is the inverse of the first. All limits of the connection formulae reproduce the right standard WKB connection formulae.

Chapter 4
Applications

4.1
Harmonic potential

The first example that we examine is the harmonic potential, given by the finite difference equation

\displaystyle\psi(x+\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)+\psi(x-\mathrm{i}\hbar,\hbar)=2(E-x^{2}+1)\psi(x,\hbar).

(4.1.1)

An unique feature of this problem is that there is a way to turn this problem in a standard WKB problem via Fourier transform: for the Fourier transform
\tilde{\psi}(p,\hbar)
related to
\psi(x,\hbar)
as

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\int\frac{\differential p}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}px}{\hbar}}\tilde{\psi}(p,\hbar),

(4.1.2)

the term
x^{2}\psi(x,\hbar)
gets converted in a double derivative term, so if
\psi(x,\hbar)
is a normalizable solution to (
4.1.1
), then
\tilde{\psi}(x,\hbar)
is a normalizable solution to

\displaystyle-\hbar^{2}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(p,\hbar)=(E-\cosh p+1)\tilde{\psi}(p,\hbar),

(4.1.3)

that is the modified Mathieu equation, studied as example in
[
25
]
. The two problems will have the same spectrum. We will examine the problem from the deformed WKB side.

In this problem, we will be able to set a method for getting the spectrum in a straightforward way. The first step is to identify the turning points of the problem. By setting
E-x^{2}+1=\pm 1
, we see that the turning points at
1
are

\displaystyle x_{2}=-\sqrt{E},\quad x_{3}=\sqrt{E},

(4.1.4)

and the turning points at
-1
are

\displaystyle x_{1}=-\sqrt{E+2},\quad x_{4}=\sqrt{E+2}.

(4.1.5)

We are ordering the turning points from left to right. The denominators of the terms
p_{n}(x)
with
n>0
even will contain integer powers of
(E-x^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
(E-x^{2}+2)^{\frac{5}{2}}
, so we will set our branch cuts for the square roots to go from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
and from
x_{3}
to
x_{4}
. We have three important cycles in the problem: the cycle between
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
(denoted as
A_{1}
), the cycle between
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
(denoted as
B
) and the cycle between
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
(denoted as
A_{2}
). The turning points also divide the
x
line in five different regions, delimited by the turning points themselves. We picture the arrangement of turning points in figure
4.1
and the cycles and branch cuts in figure
4.2
.

Figure 4.1:
Turning points for the harmonic potential, together with the regions. With reference to the terminology of subsection
1.1.2
,
III
is the classically allowed region,
II
and
IV
are classically forbidden regions and
I
and
V
are imaginary allowed regions.

Figure 4.2:
Cycles for the harmonic potential. The red wiggly lines indicate square root branch cuts, and the
B
cycle has a dashed part that lies on the second sheet of the potential.

In order to get the quantization conditions, we have to write a wavefunction ansatz and then impose normalizability in the regions
I
and
V
, that extend up to
\pm\infty
. In each region
R
, the wavefunction ansatz can be specified by giving two periodic functions
A_{R}(x,\hbar)
and
B_{R}(x,\hbar)
as

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(A_{R}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)+B_{R}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)\right).

(4.1.6)

With this convention, we can identify a wavefunction with a vector of periodic functions
(A_{R},B_{R})
in each region.

The first step is to identify the asymptotic behaviour of the wavefunctions when in regions
I
and
V
(that we will denote as the
asymptotic regions
). In order to have an
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
function, it is sufficient to look at the imaginary part of
S
, that will dictate the exponential growth or decay of the wavefunction. We have

\displaystyle\imaginary S=\int_{x_{R}}^{x}\imaginary(\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1))\differential t,

(4.1.7)

where
x_{R}
stands for either
x_{1}
in region
I
and
x_{5}
in region
V
.
As
I
and
V
are imaginary forbidden regions,
E-V(x)<-2
there, so according to our conventions we must have
\imaginary(\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1))=\pm\mathrm{i}\pi
, where also in this case the sign depends on the deformation of
\hbar
: we choose the upper sign for
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
and the lower sign for
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon
. We conclude

\displaystyle\imaginary S=\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(x-x_{R}),

(4.1.8)

so the solution
\exp\left(\mp\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\right)
is exponentially decaying in region
V
(as
x>x_{5}
there) and conversely
\exp\left(\pm\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)
is exponentially decaying in region
I
. We note already a first contrast with standard WKB, where the asymptotic behaviour is richer, given by

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\imaginary\sqrt{2(E-V(t))}\differential t,

(4.1.9)

so the behaviour at infinity can be arbitrary: in deformed WKB we only have exponential decays and growths.

The coefficients
A_{R}
and
B_{R}
also influence the asymptotic behaviour. As they are periodic functions, their
x
dependency can be expanded in a Fourier series, and it will be convenient to normalize the coefficients
a_{n}(\hbar)
and
b_{n}(\hbar)
of the series to have the expansions

\displaystyle A_{R}(x,\hbar)=\sum_{n=-\infty^{\infty}}a_{n}(\hbar)q_{x_{R}}^{n},\quad B_{R}(x,\hbar)=\sum_{n=-\infty^{\infty}}b_{n}(\hbar)q_{x_{R}}^{n}.

(4.1.10)

For
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
wavefunctions, there are constraints to impose, as the
q_{R}
factors are exponentially decaying.

We now have to deal with how to connect the coefficients
A_{R}
and
B_{R}
from different regions. This we can do using the connection formulae of chapter
3
, especially in matrix form: as an example, we take the connection from region
I
to region
II
with
\hbar
modified to
\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
. The two regions are separated by the turning point
x_{1}
, that is a turning point at
-1
with
V^{\prime}(x_{1})<1
. The appropriate matrix for the transition is
(
3.2.62
)
with
q_{x_{1}}
instead of
q_{x_{0}}
, multiplied to the left and right by matrices (
3.2.4
). We need to normalize the actions at the turning point
x_{1}
, and we will denote this as
S^{(x_{1})}
(
S_{\text{o}}
does not need normalization as it depends on derivatives of
S
). We can write (
4.1.6
) as a row-column product as

\displaystyle\psi(x,\hbar)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S_{\text{o}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{R}&B_{R}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right)\end{array}\right).

Using the transition matrices, we have

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{II}\\
B_{II}\end{array}\right)=I^{(+)}(q_{x_{1}})^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{1}{1-q_{x_{1}}^{-1}}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{q_{x_{1}}^{-1}}{q_{x_{1}}^{-1}-1}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)I^{(+)}(q_{x_{1}})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{I}\\
B_{I}\end{array}\right).

Lastly, when crossing a turning point, we obtain wavefunctions normalized at the turning point itself, in terms of
S^{(x_{1})}
1
1

1

We will not use the
T
functions of chapter
3
anymore, substituting them with
\mathrm{i}S
.
. In order to cross another turning point, we first have to normalize the action at that turning point. This can be done using the quantum periods, as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{2})}\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{2})}\right)\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)&0\\
0&\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right)\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right)\end{array}\right),

where we recall the definition of quantum periods as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(i,j)}(\hbar)=\int_{x_{i}}^{x_{j}}P_{0}(t)\differential t+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\oint_{\gamma_{i,j}}\frac{P_{2n}(t)}{(2n)!}(\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{2n}\differential t,

(4.1.26)

and
\gamma_{i,j}
can be identified as one of the cycles of figure
4.2
. The quantization conditions will impose conditions on the coefficients
A_{V}
and
B_{V}
computed in terms of
A_{I}
and
B_{I}
, and as the transition matrix obtained by following this procedure only contains the cycles as energy dependent factors, quantization conditions will be expressed as equations to be satisfied by the cycles.

4.1.1
Quantization conditions

We now come to the quantization conditions for the harmonic potential. We have to take into account the two different deformations of
\hbar
.

For this section, we will change our notation. It will be convenient to split
q_{x_{R}}
in two terms, one dependent only on
x
and the other depending only on the turning point. We will define

\displaystyle q=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}x\right),\quad q_{R}=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}x_{R}\right).

(4.1.27)

With this definition, the old
q_{x_{R}}
gets rewritten as
q/q_{R}
.

Case 1:
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon

We start from region
I
, where
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)
is exponentially decaying. This does not mean that
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)
is the only wavefunction in region
I
, as we can use factors of
q
to obtain other exponentially decaying wavefunctions involving the other exponential too. In each region, we can use a set of two coordinates to write the wavefunction. We will start in region
I
by indicating the coordinates as
C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=(A_{I}(q),B_{I}(q))
, where the upper index indicates that those are coordinates for expanding according to the basis
\left(\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right),\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{1})}\right)\right)
.
A_{I}(q)
must be a series in
q
with
a_{0}=0
, but
B_{I}(q)
can also have a
b_{0}
term (both series cannot have terms
a_{n}
or
b_{n}
with
n<0
, as they would not be normalizable).

We start with the following coordinate vector:

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{a_{0}}{q}\\
b_{0}+\frac{b_{1}}{q}\end{array}\right).

This particular vector indicates a normalizable wavefunction in region
I
. In region
II
, we apply (
4.1
) to obtain

\displaystyle C_{II}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{-\mathrm{i}a_{0}q+(b_{1}+b_{0}q)q_{1}}{q^{2}}\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{b_{1}+b_{0}q-\mathrm{i}a_{0}}{q-q_{1}}\end{array}\right).

Before passing through the point at
x_{2}
, we must write coordinates for expanding according to the basis
\left(\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{2})}\right),\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{2})}\right)\right)
. This is done with the quantum period
\Pi^{(1,2)}
:

\displaystyle C_{II}^{(x_{2})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{-\mathrm{i}a_{0}q+(b_{1}+b_{0}q)q_{1}}{q^{2}}\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{b_{1}+b_{0}q-\mathrm{i}a_{0}}{q-q_{1}}\end{array}\right).

We can now cross
x_{2}
. Proceeding in the same way, using quantum periods to change basis and transition formulas to cross the turning points, we arrive in region
V
with a complicated expression.

This expression simplifies when we impose normalizability. The coefficients in region
V
must be
(A_{V}(q),B_{V}(q))
with
A_{V}
being a series in
q
(only positive powers, due to the fact that they decay exponentially in region
V
) with possibly a constant term, while
B_{V}
must be a series in
q
(again only positive powers) with no constant term (as
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S^{(x_{5})}\right)
is not normalizable). The transitions generate coefficients of the form

\displaystyle C_{V}^{(x_{4})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{q^{3}}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}b_{1}q_{1}q_{4}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,4)}\right)}+o(q)\right),\\
\frac{1}{q^{3}}(o(q))\end{array}\right).

The coefficient of
q^{3}
must be zero: the only possibility is
b_{1}=0
. We then set
b_{1}=0
and proceed to the next term. The coefficient now reads

\displaystyle C_{V}^{(x_{4})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left(-\frac{(a_{0}+\mathrm{i}b_{0}q_{1})q_{4}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,4)}\right)}+o(q)\right),\\
\frac{1}{q^{2}}(o(q))\end{array}\right).

The coefficient of
q^{-2}
must also vanish, so we must impose
a_{0}=-\mathrm{i}b_{0}q_{x_{1}}
. As we are left only with one coefficient, we can set
b_{0}=1
, so the starting coefficient is

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{\mathrm{i}q_{1}}{q}\\
1\end{array}\right),

and after the transitions we are left with

\displaystyle C_{V}^{(x_{4})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{q}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{i}q_{4}\left(1+\exp\left(-\frac{2}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,4)}\right)}+o(q)\right),\\
o(q)\end{array}\right).

There is only one coefficient left that we have to put to zero, as all other possible coefficients are allowed. We have to impose a condition on the cycle
\Pi^{(2,3)}
, the
B-
cycle: we must have

\displaystyle\left(\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)\right)=0\implies\cos\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)=0.

(4.1.42)

The quantization condition only involves the
B-
cycle
\Pi^{(2,3)}
, even if we have other
A-
cycles in the problem. This result is the same as the one for the harmonic oscillator, and coincides with
[
43
]
(where the result was obtained without considering perturbative corrections to the cycle, approximating the quantum momentum with the classical momentum) and with
[
26
]
, where the result was derived to all orders with a different method. The quantization condition can be expressed as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)=\pm\mathrm{i},

(4.1.43)

and the
\pm
sign tracks the parity of states. To see that, we look at the coefficients in the various regions. In the asymptotic regions, we have (again, the
\pm
sign depends on the
\pm
choice in (
4.1.43
))

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{\mathrm{i}q_{1}}{q}\\
1\end{array}\right),\quad C_{V}^{(x_{4})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\pm\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}\\
\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{q_{4}}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}\end{array}\right).

For the even potential,
\pm\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}=1
and
q_{4}=q_{1}^{-1}
. Furthermore,
S^{(x_{1})}(-x,\hbar)=-S^{(x_{5})}(x,\hbar)
, meaning that the parity operation inverts the coefficients of the coordinates, and
q(-x,\hbar)=q(x,\hbar)^{-1}
. Applying parity to the coefficient
C_{V}^{(x_{4})}
then gives

\displaystyle C_{V}^{(x_{4})}\to\pm\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{\mathrm{i}q_{1}}{q}\\
1\end{array}\right),

as would be expected from an even or odd wavefunction. It is then trivial to generalize this line of reasoning to the other regions.

In (
4.1.1
), we have made a precise choice of not allowing coefficients of order
q^{-2}
or more. The reason behind this is simple, as allowing them would generate coefficients of order
q^{-4}
or more in
C_{V}^{(x_{4})}
, and the only way to remove those exponential growths in the asymptotic region is to remove coefficients of order
q^{-2}
or more in
C_{I}^{(x_{1})}
. Thus (
4.1.43
) is the complete quantization condition for the harmonic oscillator.

Case 2:
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon

In this case, the role of
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)
and
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}S\right)
are reversed: in region
I
, the first one asymptotically decays and the second one asymptotically grows, while in region
V
the opposite is true. As before, we start with the coordinate vector

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}b_{0}+\frac{b_{1}}{q}\\
\frac{a_{0}}{q}\end{array}\right).

Performing the exact same analysis as with the other deformation, we see that
b_{1}=0
and
a_{0}=-\mathrm{i}b_{0}q_{1}
. The starting vector is then (with the normalization
b_{1}=1
)

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\mathrm{i}\frac{q_{1}}{q}\\
1\end{array}\right).

After performing all the transitions, in region
V
we are left with

\displaystyle C_{V}^{(x_{4})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{q_{4}}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}\\
\pm\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)}\end{array}\right).

Again, normalizability of the overall wavefunction requires

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right)=\pm\mathrm{i},

(4.1.56)

that is the same condition as the other deformation, (
4.1.43
).

4.1.2
Computation of the quantum
B
period

In the case of the harmonic oscillator, we can give a procedure for computing arbitrary coefficients in the quantum period. We remind that the quantum periods can be expanded as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(i,j)}\simeq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Pi_{2n}^{(i,j)}}{(2n)!}(\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{2n},

(4.1.57)

where the coefficients of the series expansion are given by

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}^{(i,j)}=\int_{x_{i}}^{x_{j}}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-t^{2}+1)\differential t,\quad\Pi_{n}^{(i,j)}=\frac{1}{2}\oint_{\gamma_{i,j}}P_{2n}(t)\differential t,

(4.1.58)

as expressed in (
1.3.7
). For now, we will focus on the quantum
B-
period
\Pi^{(2,3)}
, that is the important period in the quantization condition. In this case,
x_{i}=-\sqrt{E}
and
x_{j}=\sqrt{E}
.

The.
\hbar^{0}
contribution is obtained by evaluating the integral

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}^{(2,3)}\int_{-\sqrt{E}}^{\sqrt{E}}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-t^{2}+1)\differential t=4\sqrt{E+2}\left(\mathbf{K}\left(\frac{E}{E+2}\right)-\mathbf{E}\left(\frac{E}{E+2}\right)\right).

(4.1.59)

Here
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{K}
are the elliptic functions defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(x)=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-x(\sin\theta)^{2}}}\differential\theta,\quad\mathbf{E}(x)=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sqrt{1-x(\sin\theta)^{2}}\differential\theta.

(4.1.60)

As in
[
25
]
, we can compute higher-order corrections via the Picard-Fuchs approach (see
[
36
]
for a review of its application in ordinary WKB). Up to order
\hbar^{22}
, we have found linear operators
\mathcal{O}_{n}(E)
such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}(E)p_{2n}(x)-p_{0}(x)=\left(a_{n}(E)\frac{\partial}{\partial E}+b_{n}(E)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}E}\right)p_{2n}(x)-p_{0}(x)

(4.1.61)

is a total derivative. Examples of such operators are

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{1}(E)=\frac{1}{12}\frac{\partial}{\partial E}+\frac{E+1}{6}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}E},

(4.1.62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{2}(E)=\frac{(1+E)(14-(E+2)E)}{120E^{2}(E+2)^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial E}+\frac{224-E(E+2)(4(E+2)E-145}{240E^{2}(E+2)^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}E}.

(4.1.63)

The higher order corrections are then related to the
\hbar^{0}
corrections as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(i,j)}_{2n}=\mathcal{O}_{n}(E)\Pi^{(i,j)}_{0}.

(4.1.64)

We have computed quantum corrections up to
\Pi^{(2,3)}_{18}
, and then compared the results with the ones obtained in
[
25
]
. It turns out that our periods are equal to the periods obtained for the
B-
cycle in the modified Mathieu potential, as to be expected due to the fact that the two problems are related via Fourier transform. We have used the data from
[
25
]
2
2

2

The author wishes to thank Professor Jie Gu for providing the code for computing the coefficients.
in order to compute quantum corrections up to
\Pi^{(2,3)}_{190}
.

The first test is to obtain the energy levels. In figure
4.3
, we plot the quantization condition: zeroes of the plotted function indicate energy levels. In table
4.1
, we compute zeroes of the quantization condition and compare them with numerical results. In figure
4.4
, we plot WKB wavefunctions and compare them with numerical wavefunctions. In addition, we plot the high order behaviour of the coefficients of
\Pi^{(2,3)}_{2n}
in figure
4.5
, showing that the series has zero radius of convergence.

.

\psi_{n}
for the first 4 energy levels, obtained with only the leading correction. In blue we plot the numerical wavefunctions, while in orange we plot the WKB wavefunctions. The vertical dotted lines correspond to

and

, turning points at which the WKB approximation diverges.

, at

(right). The series has zero radius of convergence.

4.1.3
Computation of the quantum
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
periods

We now turn to the computation of the other periods in the harmonic oscillator, the
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
periods. While the computation of the periods is elementary, comparison with
[
25
]
will introduce a subtlety.

We start by computing the zero order correction as

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1,2)}_{0}=\Pi^{(3,4)}_{0}=\mathrm{i}\pi\sqrt{E+2}-2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{E}\mathbf{E}\left(-\frac{2}{E}\right).

(4.1.65)

If we take the linear combination of the periods given by

\displaystyle-\Pi_{0}^{(1,2)}-\Pi_{0}^{(3,4)}=4\mathrm{i}\sqrt{E}\mathbf{E}\left(-\frac{2}{E}\right)-2\mathrm{i}\pi\sqrt{E+2}

(4.1.66)

and compare our result with the
A
period in
[
25
]
, we notice that there is a difference in our results. By noting the
A
period of
[
25
]
as
\tilde{\Pi}_{\tilde{A}}^{(0)}
, we have the equality

\displaystyle-\Pi_{0}^{(1,2)}-\Pi_{0}^{(3,4)}=2\mathrm{i}\pi\sqrt{E+2}+\tilde{\Pi}_{\tilde{A}}^{(0)}.

(4.1.67)

The factor of difference comes from the presence of the turning points at
-1
. In particular, we can observe the following relation between the associated Voros symbols, where the
0
superscript indicates that the cycles are computed with only the
\Pi_{0}^{(i,j)}
contributions to the cycles:

\displaystyle V^{(0)}_{(1,2)}V^{(0)}_{(3,4)}q_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}q_{4}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\tilde{\Pi}^{(\tilde{A})}_{0}\right).

(4.1.68)

The difference can be explained in the following way.
\tilde{\Pi}_{\tilde{A}}^{(0)}
is a
cycle
integral, while
\Pi_{0}^{(1,2)}
and
\Pi_{0}^{(3,4)}
are
line
integrals. For a cycle integral around a square root branch cut, if the line integral is convergent then it coincides with half of the cycle integral (as is the case for standard WKB, motivating the choice of regularization for the higher orders of the quantum periods, where only square root branch cuts appear). In our case, we have to consider the additional branch cuts given by the
\operatorname{arccosh}
function. The additional factors of
q_{i}
are what is needed to turn the line integrals into loop integrals, effectively canceling the
\operatorname{arccosh}
branch cut.

There is another reason why the LHS of (
4.1.68
) is a better quantity to define the cycle integrals. We can compute the higher order corrections through the quantum operator method, as for the
B-
cycles. We can verify those results via numeric integration, integrating the quantum momenta around the
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
cycles. We get the correct higher order corrections as

\displaystyle\Pi_{2n}^{(1,2)}+\Pi_{2n}^{(3,4)}=\mathcal{O}_{n}(\Pi_{0}^{(1,2)}+\Pi_{0}^{(3,4)}-2\mathrm{i}\pi\sqrt{E+2}).

(4.1.69)

This implies that relation (
4.1.68
) upgrades to all orders as

\displaystyle V_{(1,2)}V_{(3,4)}q_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}q_{4}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\tilde{\Pi}^{(\tilde{A})}\right),

(4.1.70)

and only the zero order is affected by this difference. As before, the radius of convergence of the series for
\Pi^{(1,2)}(E,\hbar)+\Pi^{(2,3)}(E,\hbar)
is zero. We estimate the radius of convergence in figure
4.6
.

Figure 4.6:
Estimation of the radius of convergence of the series for
\Pi^{(1,2)}(E,\hbar)+\Pi^{(3,4)}(E,\hbar)
, at
E=1
(left) and
E=5
(right). The series has zero radius of convergence.

For the analysis of the resurgent properties of next section, it will be convenient to make some definitions. We define

\displaystyle\Pi_{B}(E,\hbar)=-2\Pi^{(2,3)}(E,\hbar),

(4.1.71)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A}(E,\hbar)=-2(\Pi^{(1,2)}(E,\hbar)+\Pi^{(3,4)}(E,\hbar)+2\mathrm{i}\pi\sqrt{E+2}).

(4.1.72)

The extra
-2
factors are used to transform line integrals into cycle integrals. As usual, we will denote as
\Pi_{A,B}^{(0)}
the terms
\Pi_{A,B}(E,0)
, the order
\hbar^{0}
terms of the series.

4.1.4
Resurgent properties

Due to the fact that the periods coincide with the periods studied in
[
25
]
, the resurgent properties can be read from the source
3
3

3

The case that we study here is
E>0
, that is denoted as “Weak coupling region” in the cited source. A study for the case
-2<E<0
is also present there (denoted as “Strongly coupled region”), but we did not analyse it here due to the fact that it is outside of the scope of this thesis.
. Here we list the resurgent properties of the cycles of the harmonic oscillator in deformed quantum mechanics.

We will employ the familiar techniques for numerical analysis of resurgent properties, explained in detail in
[
1
]
and in Appendix
A
of this thesis. The first step for a numerical analysis is to compute the poles of the Borel-Padé approximation for the two cycles. We plot those in figures
4.7
and
4.8
.

Figure 4.7:
Poles for the Padé approximant for the period
\Pi_{A}
at
E=15
, with corrections up to
\hbar^{190}
. The poles accumulate on branch points starting at
\mathrm{i}(\pm\Pi_{B}^{(0)}+n\Pi_{A}^{(0)})
(with
n
arbitrary integer), as expected from the general theory.

Figure 4.8:
Poles for the Padé approximant for the period
\Pi_{B}
at
E=15
, with corrections up to
\hbar^{190}
. The poles accumulate on branch points starting at
\mathrm{i}(\pm\Pi_{A}^{(0)}+\Pi_{B}^{(0)})
(with
n
arbitrary integer), as expected from the general theory.

The patterns of singularities in
4.7
and
4.8
can have unexpected generalizations. First, let
\Pi_{n}=n\Pi_{A}+\Pi_{B}
. From
[
25
]
, we see that we should expect branch points of the Padé approximation to
\Pi_{A}
at the points
\mathrm{i}\Pi_{n}^{(0)}
. On the other hand, singularities of
\Pi_{B}
are expected at points
\pm\mathrm{i}\Pi_{A}^{(0)}
and
\pm\mathrm{i}\Pi_{n}^{(0)}
, with
n\neq 0
. We plot those singularities schematically in
4.9
.

Figure 4.9:
Schematic representation of the branch points for the Padé approximations of
\Pi_{A}
(above) and
\Pi_{B}
(below).

From
[
25
]
, we have the following discontinuities: letting
\alpha_{\pm}=\arg(\mathrm{i}\Pi^{(0)}_{B})
and
\theta_{n,\pm}=\arg(\mathrm{i}\Pi_{n}^{(0)})
, we have for the
A
cycle

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{0,\pm}^{+}}-S_{\theta_{0,\pm}^{-}}\right)\Pi_{A}=2\hbar\log\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{0,\pm}}(\Pi_{B})\right)\right)

(4.1.73)

and, for
n\neq 0

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{n,\pm}^{+}}-S_{\theta_{n,\pm}^{-}}\right)\Pi_{A}=-2\hbar\log\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{n,\pm}}(\Pi_{n})\right)\right).

(4.1.74)

Regarding the
B
cycle, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\pm}^{+}}-S_{\theta_{0,\pm}^{-}}\right)\Pi_{B}=4\hbar\log\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{\pm}}(\Pi_{A})\right)\right)

(4.1.75)

and, for
n\neq 0

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{n,\pm}^{+}}-S_{\theta_{n,\pm}^{-}}\right)\Pi_{B}=-2n\hbar\log\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\mathcal{S}_{\theta_{n,\pm}}(\Pi_{n})\right)\right).

(4.1.76)

The resurgent structure is rich, but very hard to probe numerically. With our limited number of coefficients (up to
\hbar^{190}
), we managed to test only formulae
(
4.1.73
)
and
(
4.1.75
)
, where we found agreement to at least 9 digits of precision for various values of
E
.

4.2
Symmetric double well

4.2.1
Ordinary WKB analysis

A classical problem in quantum mechanics is the
symmetric double well
potential, given by

\displaystyle V(x)=a(x^{2}-h)^{2},

(4.2.1)

with
a,h>0
. It is worth commenting the ordinary WKB version of this problem. We plot the potential and the turning points for ordinary WKB in figure
4.10
.

Figure 4.10:
Double well analysis for ordinary WKB, with its turning points.
(x_{1},x_{2})
and
(x_{3},x_{4})
are classically allowed regions, while
(x_{2},x_{3})
is classically forbidden.

The analysis of the double well potential is present in many sources: we refer to
[
40
]
for the analysis that inspired our method, or to
[
49
]
for a different method involving complexification of the
x
variable that gives the same result.

Perturbative quantization of the double well potential is straightforward: the quantization condition is given by

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)=0,

(4.2.2)

also using the fact that
\Pi^{(3,4)}=\Pi^{(1,2)}
. Wavefunctions can be centered in the
(x_{1},x_{2})
interval or the
(x_{3},x_{4})
interval, and they would have the same energy even if linearly independent. This poses a problem, as spectra of ordinary QM problems cannot be degenerate. This problem is removed when one considers the WKB quantization condition, given by

\displaystyle 1+\exp\left(\mp\frac{2}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right)=\pm\epsilon\mathrm{i}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right),

(4.2.3)

where the
\pm
sign depends on the deformation of
\hbar
and
\epsilon
keeps track of the parity of states, assuming values of
\pm 1
. As
\Pi^{(2,3)}
is purely imaginary and positive, the term on the RHS is of the form
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}F}
, with
F
positive: this is a non perturbative correction that is invisible to perturbation theory and breaks the degeneracy of states, creating a non degenerate even ground state and an excited odd state. In fact, neglecting the non perturbative correction we would get exactly the perturbative quantization condition. The semiclassical interpretation of this result is that the degeneracy of the ground state is broken by tunneling through the
(x_{2},x_{3})
interval. This is a very important result in ordinary WKB, and it will be worth to check if this result also holds in our deformation of WKB.

4.2.2
Deformed WKB analysis

We now turn to the symmetric double well double well in deformed WKB. In figure
4.11
we plot the potential together with its turning points, while in figure
4.12
we report the cycles on the
x
line. We refer to
4.11
for the names of the regions and the turning points.

Figure 4.11:
Turning points for the symmetric double well potential.

Figure 4.12:
Cycles for the double well potential.

In this case, we will focus on the
minimal chamber
, the part of the spectrum where all turning points are real (so
E+2<V(0)
). We will make use of the symmetry of the potential: for the turning points we have

\displaystyle x_{i}=-x_{9-i},\quad i=1,...,4\implies q_{i}=q_{9-i}^{-1},\quad i=1,...,4,

(4.2.4)

while for the cycles we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{(x_{i},x_{i+1})}=\Pi^{(x_{9-i},x_{8-i})},\quad i=1,2,3.

(4.2.5)

Werecall the names to the Voros symbols:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{V^{(1,2)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(1,2)}\right),\quad{V^{(2,3)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(2,3)}\right),\\
&{V^{(3,4)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(3,4)}\right),\quad{V^{(4,5)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\Pi^{(4,5)}\right).\end{aligned}

(4.2.6)

In our analysis, we will assume that all turning points are real,
E+2<V(0)
. Inspired by subsection
4.1.4
, we interpret periods (
4.2.6
) as line integrals. As we have seen, the objects with good resurgent properties are the cycle integrals, in which the branch cut associated to the
\operatorname{arccosh}(x)
discontinuity at
x=-1
is removed via multiplication by the appropriate
q
factor, centered at a turning point at
-1
. The general definition of loop integrals is difficult in this context, as we do not have ways to test if our definition is actually advantageous. Nevertheless, we will give a definition of cycle integrals. As the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon
influences the behaviour of the turning points at
-1
, we have to give different loop definitions. For the
-
case, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{V_{A_{1}}}^{(-)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q_{1}}}V^{{(1,2)}},\quad{V_{B_{2}}}^{(-)}=V^{(2,3)},\\
&\quad V_{A_{2}}^{(-)}=\sqrt{q_{4}}V^{(3,4)},\quad V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}=\sqrt{\frac{q_{4}}{q_{5}}}V^{(4,5)}=q_{4}V^{(4,5)}.\end{aligned}

(4.2.7)

For the
+
case, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{V_{A_{1}}}^{(+)}={\sqrt{q_{1}}}V^{{(1,2)}},\quad{V_{B_{2}}}^{(+)}=V^{(2,3)},\\
&\quad V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q_{4}}}V^{(3,4)},\quad V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}=\sqrt{\frac{q_{5}}{q_{4}}}V^{(4,5)}=\frac{1}{q_{4}}V^{(4,5)}.\end{aligned}

(4.2.8)

Some notes about those definitions:

•

Only
q_{1}
and
q_{4}
appear in those definitions, as those are the only turning points at
-1
. In particular, we have used
q_{4}=q_{5}^{-1}
for even potentials to get rid of the non independent point.

•

We have redefined via multiplication by
q_{i}^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}
if the turning point at
-1
is on the left side of the line
(i,j)
, by
q_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}
if the turning point at
1
is on the right side of
(i,j)
(the upper sign refers to the
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
deformation, the lower sign to the
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon
deformation). This is consistent with subsection
4.1.4
.

•

We have used the nomenclature of figure
4.12
for the cycles.

•

We will write the quantization conditions both in terms of the line integrals (easier to compute, more relevant to WKB) and the cycle integrals (possibly more relevant for resurgent analysis).

Before giving the most general quantization condition, there is a particular quantization condition that we analyse.

Degenerate spectrum

We start with the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
and with the coordinate vector

\displaystyle C_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\mathrm{i}\frac{q_{1}}{q}\\
1\end{array}\right).

Such a state can belong to the discrete spectrum. To see that, we first go through all transitions and examine
C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}
. At order
q^{-2}
, this is given by

\displaystyle C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}q_{4}\left(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}\right)}{q_{1}{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}}+o(q)\right)\\
-\frac{q_{1}q_{4}\left({V^{(1,2)}}^{2}-1\right)\left({V^{(2,3)}}^{2}+1\right){V^{(4,5)}}}{{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}}+o(q)\end{array}\right).

To have a normalizable state, we must have
1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}=0
. By imposing this condition, we can simplify the next term in
C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}
and get

\displaystyle C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{q}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}V_{C}\left(1+q_{4}^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}^{2}\right)}{q_{1}q_{4}^{2}{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}}+o(q)\right)\\
o(q)\end{array}\right).

Another necessary condition is
1+q_{4}^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}^{2}=0
. The two conditions are solved by

\displaystyle{V^{(2,3)}}=s_{1}\mathrm{i},\quad{V^{(4,5)}}=s_{2}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{q_{4}},

(4.2.15)

where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are signs, taking values
\pm 1
. In terms of the loop integrals, those can be written as

\displaystyle V_{B_{1}}^{(-)}=s_{1}\mathrm{i},\quad V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}=s_{2}\mathrm{i}.

(4.2.16)

At
+\infty
, we have

\displaystyle C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}=s_{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-q\\
\mathrm{i}q^{2}q_{1}\end{array}\right).

Due to the fact that
q
and
q^{2}
decay exponentially faster than the actions, the wavefunction described by the vectors
C
is normalizable. But this is not the only wavefunction admissible at this energy: if we start with

\displaystyle D_{I}^{(x_{1})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{i}\frac{q_{1}}{q^{2}}\\
-\frac{1}{q}\end{array}\right).

and impose the
same
quantization conditions (
4.2.15
), we get in the
+\infty
region

\displaystyle D_{IX}^{(x_{8})}=s_{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
-\mathrm{i}q_{1}q^{2}\end{array}\right).

This also indicates a normalizable wavefunction,
at the same energy
of the previous one and
linearly independent
from the previous one. The energy level given by imposing (
4.2.15
) is then
degenerate
. This is a stark contrast with respect to the result of standard quantum mechanics where degenerate energy levels are forbidden. Furthermore, the wavefunctions can be organized as even and odd wavefunctions: remembering that the parity operator on vectors in region
IX
consists in reversing the vector and imposing
q\to q^{-1}
, we can easily verify that for
s_{2}=1
the wavefunction obtained from
C_{I}^{(x_{1})}+D_{I}^{(x_{1})}
is even while the wavefunction obtained from
C_{I}^{(x_{1})}-D_{I}^{(x_{1})}
is odd, and the opposite is valid for
s_{2}=-1
. For
\hbar\to\hbar+\mathrm{i}\epsilon
the same result holds, with a slightly modified quantization condition:

\displaystyle{V^{(2,3)}}=s_{1}\mathrm{i},\quad{V^{(4,5)}}=s_{2}\mathrm{i}q_{4}.

(4.2.23)

In terms of the loop integrals, those quantization conditions become

\displaystyle V_{B_{1}}^{(+)}=s_{1}\mathrm{i},\quad V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}=s_{2}\mathrm{i}.

(4.2.24)

The result that we got coincides with the result in
[
43
]
at order
\hbar^{0}
, as at that order the cycles are

\displaystyle{V^{(2,3)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\int_{x_{2}}^{x_{3}}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1)\differential t\right),

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle{V^{(4,5)}}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\int_{x_{4}}^{x_{4}}\operatorname{arccosh}(V(t)-E-1)\differential t\right)\exp\left(\pm\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}x_{4}\right):

(4.2.26)

in the second line we have used the fact that in the imaginary allowed zone we can use
\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t))=\operatorname{arccosh}(V(t)-E)\pm\mathrm{i}\hbar
, depending on the deformation of
\hbar
. The quantization condition at this level is

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{x_{2}}^{x_{3}}\operatorname{arccosh}(E-V(t)+1)\differential t\right)=0,

(4.2.27)

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{x_{4}}^{x_{4}}\operatorname{arccosh}(V(t)-E-1)\differential t\right)=0.

(4.2.28)

Our result is an all-order generalization of the result
[
43
]
, showing that degenerate states exist even if one takes all orders into account, and gives a prescription to compute higher-order corrections of the energy levels.

It might be possible that there are no energies such as (
4.2.15
) or (
4.2.23
) holds. In fact, this is generally not true for all values of
a
and
h
, but there are values of the parameters that allow the existence of those energy levels. In
[
26
]
the same was argued, together with the existence of degenerate energy levels. In fact, those energy levels were identified with the energy levels of Toda lattices, a comparison that we will expand in the next section. For now, we give numerical evidence for the existence of such states: in figure
4.13
we show a combination of
a,h
and
\hbar
producing such a state, while in figure
4.14
we show a list of possible
h
at fixed
a
at which we have the degenerate eigenvalues that we are examining. The quantum mechanics interpretation of those eigenstates is that there are points
(a(\hbar),h(\hbar))
at which quantum tunnelling is suppressed, so the degeneracy breaking due to tunnelling does not happen.

Figure 4.13:
Quantization condition (
4.2.15
) or (
4.2.23
) at
\hbar=1
,
a=\frac{1}{2}
,
h=9.11
. The blue line is the LHS of (
4.2.27
), the orange line is the LHS of (
4.2.28
). There is only one energy level from the Toda quantization condition, obtained when both functions are zero at the same energy.

Figure 4.14:
A collection of degenerate energy levels arranged in an
(h,E)
plot, with
\hbar=1
and
a=\frac{1}{2}
. Each point corresponds to a degenerate energy level, that exists only for the value of
h
and takes value
E
. We have also fitted a line to show that some points arrange on a line: this can be interpreted as two distinct energy levels
E_{n}(h)
and
E_{n+1}(h)
, joining only at the points
h
for which we have degeneracy
E_{n}(h)=E_{n+1}(h)
.

General quantization condition

Degenerate energy levels are not the only admissible energy levels for the double well. It turns out that the spectrum is way richer, and a more general quantization condition can be found with a more general starting wavefunction.

Starting with the deformation
\hbar\to\hbar-\mathrm{i}\epsilon
, we start in region
I
with the wavefunction indicated by

\displaystyle C_{I}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{a_{1}}{q}+\frac{a_{2}}{q^{2}}\\
b_{0}+\frac{b_{1}}{q}+\frac{b_{2}}{q^{2}}\end{array}\right).

Getting the quantization condition is now a very computationally intensive procedure that we performed using a computer algebra system (in our case, Mathematica 12.1.0) and removing all
q^{-n}
contributions in
C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}
with
n>0
, together with the
q^{0}
contribution in the second spot of
C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}
. We use four out of the five coefficients to eliminate all terms of order
q^{-n}
from
C_{IX}^{(x_{8})}
, and at the end we are left with a vector whose second component is
o(q)
(indicating a normalizable contribution), and the first component still has an order
q^{-1}
component that is proportional to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &QC_{-}=q_{4}^{6}{V^{(4,5)}}^{4}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2})^{3}\left(1-\frac{q_{1}^{2}{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}}{q_{2}^{2}}\right)+\\
+&q_{1}^{2}{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}({V^{(2,3)}}^{2}(q_{4}^{3}q_{3}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2})^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}^{2}+{V^{(3,4)}}^{4}(1+q_{4}^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}^{2})^{2}-\\
-&{V^{(3,4)}}^{2}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2})(1+q_{4}^{2}{V^{(4,5)}}^{2})^{2})).\end{aligned}

(4.2.31)

The quantization condition is
QC_{-}=0
. In terms of loop integrals, this quantization condition becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &QC_{-}=q_{4}^{2}{V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}}^{4}(1+{V_{B_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2})^{3}\left(1-\frac{q_{1}^{4}{V_{A_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2}}{q_{2}^{2}}\right)+\\
+&q_{1}^{4}{V_{A_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2}({V_{B_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2}(q_{4}q_{3}(1+{V_{B_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2})^{2}{V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}}^{2}+\frac{1}{q_{4}^{2}}{V_{A_{2}}^{(-)}}^{4}(1+{V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}}^{2})^{2}-\\
-&\frac{1}{{q_{4}}^{2}}{V_{A_{2}}^{(-)}}^{2}(1+{V_{B_{1}}^{(-)}}^{2})(1+{V_{B_{2}}^{(-)}}^{2})^{2})).\end{aligned}

(4.2.32)

With this quantization condition the degenerate states are also included, as substituting
(
4.2.15
)
gives
QC_{-}=0
. In this most general quantization condition, we can see the role of the non perturbative corrections
{V^{(1,2)}}
and
{V^{(3,4)}}
, that bring to the quantization condition terms of order
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}P\right)
, with
P
real positive. The same procedure holds for the positive deformation, where we obtain from the starting vector

\displaystyle C_{I}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a_{0}+\frac{a_{1}}{q}+\frac{a_{2}}{q^{2}}\\
\frac{b_{1}}{q}+\frac{b_{2}}{q^{2}}\end{array}\right),

the quantization term

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &QC_{+}=q_{2}^{2}q_{4}^{2}{V^{(1,2)}}^{2}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2})^{3}{V^{(3,4)}}^{4}{V^{(4,5)}}^{4}-q_{1}^{2}q_{4}^{2}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2})^{3}{V^{(3,4)}}^{4}{V^{(4,5)}}^{4}+\\
&+q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}(q_{4}^{4}(1-(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}){V^{(3,4)}}^{2})+2q_{4}^{2}+\\
&+q_{4}^{2}((1+V_{B}^{2}){V^{(3,4)}}^{2}(-2+q_{4}^{2}(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}){V^{(3,4)}}^{2})){V^{(4,5)}}^{2}-\\
&-(-1+(1+{V^{(2,3)}}^{2}){V^{(3,4)}}^{2}){V^{(4,5)}}^{4}),\end{aligned}

(4.2.35)

and the quantization condition is
QC_{+}=0
. In terms of loop integrals, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &QC_{+}=\frac{q_{2}^{2}q_{4}^{2}}{q_{1}}{V_{A_{1}}^{(+)}}^{2}(1+q_{4}^{2}{V_{B_{1}}^{(+)}}^{2})^{3}{V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}}^{4}{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{4}-\\
&-q_{1}^{2}q_{4}^{4}(1+{V_{B_{1}}^{(+)}}^{2})^{3}{V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}}^{4}{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{4}+q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2}(q_{4}^{4}(1-q_{4}(1+{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2}){V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2})+2q_{4}^{2}+\\
&+q_{4}^{5}((1+{V_{B_{1}}^{(+)}}^{2}){{V_{A_{2}}}^{(+)}}^{2}(-2+q_{4}^{3}(1+{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2}){V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2})){V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2}-\\
&-(-1+(1+q_{4}{V_{B_{1}}^{(+)}}^{2}){V_{A_{2}}^{(+)}}^{2})q_{4}^{4}{V_{B_{2}}^{(+)}}^{4}),\end{aligned}

(4.2.36)

The two quantization conditions are very complicated, but they do bring to new states. We now compare the spectrum obtained with
(
4.2.31
)
with numerical results from appendix
B
. We choose
a=\frac{1}{2}
and
h=10
, for which we have
E_{0}=4.09799
and
E_{1}=4.09932
. The results of our quantization condition (with corrections up to
\hbar^{4}
) are reported in table
4.2
. As we can see, we get a good accuracy, improved by including further corrections. Unfortunately, due to the fact that the energy states are almost degenerate, we are not able to find the two states at the precision that we employed. Improving the precision of this computation is a future line of research.
